[1100](https://whc.unesco.org/en/list/1100) |\n|  |\n|  |\n\n**Rio de Janeiro**,[[c]](#cite_note-9) also known simply as **Rio**,[[7]](#cite_note-10) is the capital and largest city of the [state of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_(state) \"Rio de Janeiro (state)\"). It is the [second-largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Brazil_by_population \"List of cities in Brazil by population\") in Brazil after [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo \"São Paulo\"), and the largest by international tourists. It has a population of 6 million in the city proper, and 13 million people in its metropolitan area as of 2025. It is also the [sixth-most-populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largest_cities_in_the_Americas \"Largest cities in the Americas\") in the [Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas \"Americas\").\n\nFounded in 1565, the city was initially the seat of the [Captaincy of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Captaincy of Rio de Janeiro\"), a domain of the [Portuguese Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire \"Portuguese Empire\"). In 1763, it became the capital of the [State of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Brazil \"State of Brazil\"). In 1808, when the [Portuguese Royal Court moved to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_Court_to_Brazil \"Transfer of the Portuguese Court to Brazil\"), Rio de Janeiro became the seat of the court of Queen [Maria I of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_I_of_Portugal \"Maria I of Portugal\"). Under the leadership of her son, Prince Regent [John of Braganza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_VI_of_Portugal \"John VI of Portugal\"), Maria raised Brazil to the dignity of a kingdom within the [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil, and Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves \"United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves\"). Rio remained as the capital of the [pluricontinental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluricontinentalism \"Pluricontinentalism\") monarchy until 1822, when the [Brazilian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_War_of_Independence \"Brazilian War of Independence\") began. This is the only instance in modern history that the capital of a colonizing country officially shifted to a city in one of its colonies. Rio de Janeiro subsequently served as the capital of the [Empire of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil \"Empire of Brazil\") until 1889, and then the capital of republican Brazil until 1960, when the capital was moved to [Brasília](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brasília \"Brasília\").\n\nRio de Janeiro has the second largest municipal [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_domestic_product \"Gross domestic product\") in the country,[[8]](#cite_note-IBGE_PIB_Ranking-11) and 30th-largest in the world as of 2008,[[9]](#cite_note-Richest_Cities-12) estimated at [R$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_real \"Brazilian real\")343 billion. It is home to the headquarters of major Brazilian oil, mining, and telecommunications companies, including two of the country's largest corporations, [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras \"Petrobras\") and [Vale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vale_S.A. \"Vale S.A.\"), and Latin America's largest telemedia conglomerate, [Grupo Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupo_Globo \"Grupo Globo\"). The home of many universities and institutes, it is the second-largest center of research and development in Brazil, accounting for 17 percent of national scientific output according to 2005 data.[[10]](#cite_note-Unicamp_2005-13) Despite the perception of a high crime rate, the city actually has a lower [incidence of crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Brazil \"Crime in Brazil\") than most state capitals in Brazil.[[11]](#cite_note-14)\n\nRio de Janeiro is one of the most visited cities in the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere \"Southern Hemisphere\") and is known for its natural settings, [carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Carnival \"Rio Carnival\"), [samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba \"Samba\"), [bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova \"Bossa nova\"), and beaches such as [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\"), [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Copacabana, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema \"Ipanema\"), and [Leblon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leblon \"Leblon\").[[12]](#cite_note-15) In addition to the beaches, other landmarks include the statue of [*Christ the Redeemer*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_the_Redeemer_(statue) \"Christ the Redeemer (statue)\") atop [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado \"Corcovado\") mountain, named one of the [New Seven Wonders of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New7Wonders_of_the_World \"New7Wonders of the World\"); [Sugarloaf Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain \"Sugarloaf Mountain\") with its [cable car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Cable_Car \"Sugarloaf Cable Car\"); the *[Sambódromo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambadrome_Marquês_de_Sapucaí \"Sambadrome Marquês de Sapucaí\")*, a permanent grandstand-lined parade avenue which is used during Carnival; and [Maracanã Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracanã_Stadium \"Maracanã Stadium\"), one of the [world's largest football stadiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_association_football_stadiums_by_capacity \"List of association football stadiums by capacity\"). Rio de Janeiro was the host of the [2016 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics \"2016 Summer Olympics\") and [Paralympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Paralympics \"2016 Summer Paralympics\"), making the city the first South American and [Portuguese-speaking city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_where_Portuguese_is_an_official_language \"List of countries where Portuguese is an official language\") to host these events, and the third time that the [Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Games \"Olympic Games\") were held in a Southern Hemisphere city.[[13]](#cite_note-16) The Maracanã Stadium held the finals of the [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_FIFA_World_Cup \"1950 FIFA World Cup\") and [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_FIFA_World_Cup \"2014 FIFA World Cup\") FIFA World Cups and the [2013 FIFA Confederations Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_FIFA_Confederations_Cup \"2013 FIFA Confederations Cup\"). The city also hosted the [XV Pan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pan_American_Games \"2007 Pan American Games\") in 2007, and the [G20 summit in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_G20_Rio_de_Janeiro_summit \"2024 G20 Rio de Janeiro summit\"), and will host the [FIFA Women's World Cup in 2027](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2027_FIFA_Women's_World_Cup \"2027 FIFA Women's World Cup\").[[14]](#cite_note-17)[[15]](#cite_note-18)\n\n## History\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=1 \"Edit section: History\")]\n\nMain articles: [History of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"History of Rio de Janeiro\") and [Timeline of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Timeline of Rio de Janeiro\")\n\nHistorical affiliations\n\n> [![Portugal](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Flag_of_Portugal_%281640%29.svg/40px-Flag_of_Portugal_%281640%29.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal \"Portugal\") [Portuguese Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire \"Portuguese Empire\") 1565–1815  \n> ![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom_of_Portugal%2C_Brazil%2C_and_the_Algarves_%281815-1825%29.svg/40px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom_of_Portugal%2C_Brazil%2C_and_the_Algarves_%281815-1825%29.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves \"United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves\") 1815–1822  \n> ![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Flag_of_Brazil_%281822%E2%80%931853%29.svg/40px-Flag_of_Brazil_%281822%E2%80%931853%29.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) [Empire of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil \"Empire of Brazil\") 1822–1889  \n> [![Brazil](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/05/Flag_of_Brazil.svg/40px-Flag_of_Brazil.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil \"Brazil\") [Republic of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil \"Brazil\") 1889–present\n\n### Pre-Cabraline period\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=2 \"Edit section: Pre-Cabraline period\")]\n\nMain article: [Pre-Cabraline history of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Cabraline_history_of_Brazil \"Pre-Cabraline history of Brazil\")\n\nThe region of Rio was inhabited by the [Tupi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupi_people \"Tupi people\"), [Puri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purí_people \"Purí people\"), [Botocudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botocudo_people \"Botocudo people\") and [Maxakalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxakalí_language \"Maxakalí language\") peoples.[[16]](#cite_note-19)\n\n### Colonial period\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=3 \"Edit section: Colonial period\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Pal%C3%A1cio_Pedro_Ernesto_-_Funda%C3%A7%C3%A3o_da_Cidade.jpg/250px-Pal%C3%A1cio_Pedro_Ernesto_-_Funda%C3%A7%C3%A3o_da_Cidade.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Palácio_Pedro_Ernesto_-_Fundação_da_Cidade.jpg)\n\nFounding of Rio de Janeiro on 1 March 1565. The painting depicts the Governor General of Brazil, [Mem de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mem_de_Sá \"Mem de Sá\"), presenting the [key to the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Key_to_the_city \"Key to the city\") to the *alcaide*.\n\nEuropeans first encountered [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay \"Guanabara Bay\") on 1 January 1502 (hence Rio de Janeiro, \"January River\"), during a [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people \"Portuguese people\") expedition under explorer [Gaspar de Lemos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaspar_de_Lemos \"Gaspar de Lemos\"), captain of a ship in [Pedro Álvares Cabral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Álvares_Cabral \"Pedro Álvares Cabral\")'s fleet, or under [Gonçalo Coelho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonçalo_Coelho \"Gonçalo Coelho\").[[17]](#cite_note-20) Allegedly the Florentine explorer [Amerigo Vespucci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amerigo_Vespucci \"Amerigo Vespucci\") participated as an observer at the invitation of [King Manuel I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_I_of_Portugal \"Manuel I of Portugal\") in the same expedition.\n\nIn 1555, one of the islands of Guanabara Bay, now called [Villegagnon Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villegagnon_Island \"Villegagnon Island\"), was occupied by 500 French colonists under the French [admiral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admiral \"Admiral\") [Nicolas Durand de Villegaignon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Durand_de_Villegaignon \"Nicolas Durand de Villegaignon\"). Consequently, Villegagnon built [Fort Coligny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Coligny \"Fort Coligny\") on the island when attempting to establish the [France Antarctique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_Antarctique \"France Antarctique\") colony. Eventually, this French settlement became too much of a threat to the established Portuguese colony, and in 1560 the order was made to get rid of them. A years-long military aggression was then initiated by the new Governor General of Brazil [Mem de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mem_de_Sá \"Mem de Sá\"), and later continued by his nephew [Estácio de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estácio_de_Sá \"Estácio de Sá\"). On 20 January 1567, a final defeat was imposed on the French forces, and they were decisively expelled from Brazil for good.\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Cais_do_Valongo_e_da_Imperatriz.jpg/250px-Cais_do_Valongo_e_da_Imperatriz.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cais_do_Valongo_e_da_Imperatriz.jpg)\n\nBetween 500 thousand and one million Africans were trafficked at [Valongo Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valongo_Wharf \"Valongo Wharf\") through the [Atlantic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade \"Atlantic slave trade\")[[18]](#cite_note-21)\n\nThe city of Rio de Janeiro proper was founded on 1 March 1565 by the Portuguese, led by Estácio de Sá, including [Antônio de Mariz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antônio_de_Mariz?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Antônio de Mariz (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antônio%20de%20Mariz \"pt:Antônio de Mariz\")]. It was named *São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro*, in honor of St. Sebastian, the [saint who was the namesake and patron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint's_name \"Saint's name\") of the Portuguese then-monarch [Sebastião](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_of_Portugal \"Sebastian of Portugal\"). *Rio de Janeiro* was the name of Guanabara Bay. Until early in the 18th century, the city was threatened or invaded by several mostly French pirates and buccaneers, such as [Jean-François Duclerc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-François_Duclerc \"Jean-François Duclerc\") and [René Duguay-Trouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/René_Duguay-Trouin \"René Duguay-Trouin\").[[19]](#cite_note-22)\n\nIn the late 17th century, [Bandeirantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandeirantes \"Bandeirantes\") discovered gold and diamonds in the neighboring [captaincy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy \"Captaincy\") of [Minas Gerais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minas_Gerais \"Minas Gerais\"), thus Rio de Janeiro became a much more practical port for exporting diversified sources of wealth (gold, precious stones, besides sugar) than [Salvador, Bahia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador,_Bahia \"Salvador, Bahia\"), much farther northeast. On 27 January 1763,[[20]](#cite_note-RobinsonRobinson2014-23) the colonial administration in Portuguese America was moved from Salvador to Rio de Janeiro. The city remained primarily a colonial capital until 1808, when the Portuguese royal family and most of the associated [Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon \"Lisbon\") nobles, fleeing from [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_I \"Napoleon I\")'s invasion of Portugal, [moved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_Court_to_Brazil \"Transfer of the Portuguese Court to Brazil\") to Rio de Janeiro.\n\n### Portuguese royal period\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=4 \"Edit section: Portuguese royal period\")]\n\nMain article: [Transfer of the Portuguese court to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_court_to_Brazil \"Transfer of the Portuguese court to Brazil\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Nicolas-Antoine_Taunay.jpg/250px-Nicolas-Antoine_Taunay.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nicolas-Antoine_Taunay.jpg)\n\n*View of the bay and the entry of the city of Rio as seen from the terrace of the convent of Santo Antônio*, by [Nicolas-Antoine Taunay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas-Antoine_Taunay \"Nicolas-Antoine Taunay\") (1816)\n\nThe kingdom's capital was transferred to the city, which, thus, became the only European capital outside of [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe \"Europe\"). As there was no physical space or urban structure to accommodate hundreds of noblemen who arrived suddenly, many inhabitants were evicted from their homes.[[21]](#cite_note-24) In the first decade, several educational establishments were created, such as the Military Academy, the Royal School of Sciences, Arts and Crafts and the [Imperial Academy of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Imperial_de_Belas_Artes \"Academia Imperial de Belas Artes\"), as well as the [National Library of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil \"National Library of Brazil\") – with the largest collection in [Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_America \"Latin America\")[[22]](#cite_note-BNB-25) – and The [Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Botanical_Garden \"Rio de Janeiro Botanical Garden\"). The first printed newspaper in Brazil, the *[Gazeta do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazeta_do_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Gazeta do Rio de Janeiro\")*, came into circulation during this period.[[23]](#cite_note-26) When Brazil was elevated to Kingdom in 1815, it became the capital of the [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves \"United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves\") until the return of the [Portuguese Royal Family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Royal_Family \"Portuguese Royal Family\") to [Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon \"Lisbon\") in 1821, but remained as capital of the [Kingdom of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Brazil \"Kingdom of Brazil\").[[24]](#cite_note-HCRJ-27)\n\nFrom the colonial period until the first independent era, Rio de Janeiro was a city of enslaved people. There was a large influx of [African slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Brazil \"Slavery in Brazil\") to Rio de Janeiro: in 1819, there were 145,000 slaves in the captaincy. In 1840, the number of enslaved people reached 220,000.[[25]](#cite_note-28) Between 1811 and 1831, 500,000 to a million enslaved people arrived in Rio de Janeiro through [Valongo Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valongo_Wharf \"Valongo Wharf\"), which is now a [World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site \"World Heritage Site\").[[26]](#cite_note-29) The Port of Rio de Janeiro was the largest enslaved people port in America.[[27]](#cite_note-30)\n\n### Imperial period\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=5 \"Edit section: Imperial period\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Nicola_Antonio_Facchinetti_-_Enseada_do_Botafogo.JPG/250px-Nicola_Antonio_Facchinetti_-_Enseada_do_Botafogo.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nicola_Antonio_Facchinetti_-_Enseada_do_Botafogo.JPG)\n\nBotafogo Bay in 1869\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Rio_de_janeiro_1889_01.jpg/250px-Rio_de_janeiro_1889_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rio_de_janeiro_1889_01.jpg)\n\nBotafogo Bay in 1889\n\nWhen [Prince Pedro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_I_of_Brazil \"Pedro I of Brazil\") proclaimed the [independence of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Declaration_of_Independence \"Brazilian Declaration of Independence\") in 1822, he decided to keep Rio de Janeiro as the capital of his [new empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil \"Empire of Brazil\") while the place was enriched with sugar cane agriculture in the Campos region and, especially, with the new coffee cultivation in the [Paraíba Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraíba_Valley \"Paraíba Valley\").[[24]](#cite_note-HCRJ-27) In order to separate the province from the capital of the Empire, the city was converted in Neutral Municipality in 1834, passing the [province of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_(state) \"Rio de Janeiro (state)\") to have [Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niterói \"Niterói\") as capital.[[24]](#cite_note-HCRJ-27)\n\nAs a political center of the country, Rio concentrated the political-partisan life of the Empire. It was the main stage of the abolitionist and republican movements in the last half of the 19th century.[[24]](#cite_note-HCRJ-27) At that time, the number of enslaved people was drastically reduced, and the city was developed, with modern drains, animal trams, train stations crossing the city, gas and electric lighting, telephone and telegraph wiring, water and river plumbing.[[24]](#cite_note-HCRJ-27) Rio continued as the capital of Brazil after 1889, when the monarchy was [replaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_Republic_(Brazil) \"Proclamation of the Republic (Brazil)\") by a republic.\n\nOn 6 February 1889, the Bangu Textile Factory was founded, with the name of Industrial Progress Company of Brazil (Companhia Progresso Industrial do Brasil). The factory was officially opened on 8 March 1893, in a complex with varying architectural styles like [Italianate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italianate_architecture \"Italianate architecture\"), [Neo-Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival_architecture \"Gothic Revival architecture\") and a tower in [Mansard Roof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansard_roof \"Mansard roof\") style. After the opening in 1893, workers from [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain \"Great Britain\") arrived in Bangu to work in the textile factory. The old farms became worker villages with red-brick houses, and a neo-Gothic church was created, which still exists as the Saint Sebastian and Saint Cecilia Parish Church. Street cinemas and cultural buildings also appeared. In May 1894, [Thomas Donohoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Donohoe \"Thomas Donohoe\"), a British worker from [Busby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Busby,_East_Renfrewshire \"Busby, East Renfrewshire\"), Scotland, arrived in Bangu.[[28]](#cite_note-31)\n\nDonohoe was amazed to discover that there was absolutely no knowledge of football among Brazilians. So he wrote to his wife, Elizabeth, asking her to bring a football when she joined him. And shortly after her arrival, in September 1894, the first football match in Brazil took place in the field beside the textile factory. It was a five-a-side match between British workers, and took place six months before the first game organized by [Charles Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_William_Miller \"Charles William Miller\") in São Paulo. However, the Bangu Football Club was not formally created until 1904.[[29]](#cite_note-32)\n\n### Republican period\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=6 \"Edit section: Republican period\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Rio_de_Janeiro_ca1910s_photo_from_USA_Library_of_Congress_19301u.jpg/250px-Rio_de_Janeiro_ca1910s_photo_from_USA_Library_of_Congress_19301u.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rio_de_Janeiro_ca1910s_photo_from_USA_Library_of_Congress_19301u.jpg)\n\nRio de Janeiro, c. 1910s\n\nAt the time Brazil's [Old Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Brazilian_Republic \"First Brazilian Republic\") was established, the city lacked [urban planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_planning \"Urban planning\") and [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation \"Sanitation\"), which helped spread several diseases, such as [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever \"Yellow fever\"), [dysentery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dysentery \"Dysentery\"), [variola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variola \"Variola\"), [tuberculosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuberculosis \"Tuberculosis\") and even [black death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_death \"Black death\"). [Pereira Passos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Pereira_Passos \"Francisco Pereira Passos\"), who was named mayor in 1902, imposed reforms to modernize the city, demolishing the [cortiços](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortiço \"Cortiço\") where most of the poor population lived. These people then moved to live in the city's hills, creating the first [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas \"Favelas\").[[30]](#cite_note-33) Inspired by the city of [Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris \"Paris\"), Passos built the [Municipal Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatro_Municipal_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Theatro Municipal (Rio de Janeiro)\"), the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes \"Museu Nacional de Belas Artes\") and the [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil \"National Library of Brazil\") in the city's center; brought [electric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_power \"Electric power\") to Rio and created larger avenues to adapt the city to [automobiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automobiles \"Automobiles\").[[31]](#cite_note-34) Passos also named [Oswaldo Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswaldo_Cruz \"Oswaldo Cruz\") as Director General of Public Health. Cruz's plans to clean the city of diseases included compulsory vaccination of the entire population and forced entry into houses to kill mosquitoes and rats. The people of the city rebelled against Cruz's policy, in what would be known as the [Vaccine Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_Revolt \"Vaccine Revolt\").[[32]](#cite_note-35)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/Aqueduto_da_Carioca%2C_Santa_Teresa_e_Gl%C3%B3ria_%28007A5P3F13-029%29.jpg/250px-Aqueduto_da_Carioca%2C_Santa_Teresa_e_Gl%C3%B3ria_%28007A5P3F13-029%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aqueduto_da_Carioca,_Santa_Teresa_e_Glória_(007A5P3F13-029).jpg)\n\n[Carioca Aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Aqueduct \"Carioca Aqueduct\") in the 1920s\n\nIn 1910, Rio saw the [Revolt of the Lash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolt_of_the_Lash \"Revolt of the Lash\"), where [Afro-Brazilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilian \"Afro-Brazilian\") crew members in the [Brazilian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Navy \"Brazilian Navy\") mutinied against the heavy use of [corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment \"Corporal punishment\"), which was similar to the punishment enslaved people received. The mutineers took control of the [battleship *Minas Geraes*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_battleship_Minas_Geraes \"Brazilian battleship Minas Geraes\") and threatened to fire on the city. Another military revolt occurred in 1922, the [Copacabana Fort revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_Fort_revolt \"Copacabana Fort revolt\"), a march against the Old Republic's [coronelism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronelism \"Coronelism\") and [café com leite politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Café_com_leite_politics \"Café com leite politics\"). This revolt marked the beginning of [Tenentism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenentism \"Tenentism\"), a movement which resulted in the [Revolution of 1930](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_1930 \"Revolution of 1930\") that started the [Vargas Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vargas_Era \"Vargas Era\").\n\nUntil the early years of the 20th century, the city was largely limited to the neighborhood now known as the historic city center (see below), on the mouth of Guanabara Bay. The city's center of gravity began to shift south and west to the so-called [Zona Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Zone_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"South Zone (Rio de Janeiro)\") (South Zone) in the early part of the 20th century, when the first tunnel was built under the mountains between [Botafogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo \"Botafogo\") and the neighborhood that is now known as [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Copacabana (Rio de Janeiro)\"). Expansion of the city to the north and south was facilitated by the consolidation and electrification of Rio's streetcar transit system after 1905.[[33]](#cite_note-36) Botafogos natural environment, combined with the fame of the [Copacabana Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_Palace \"Copacabana Palace\") Hotel, the luxury hotel of the Americas in the 1930s, helped Rio to gain the reputation it still holds today as a beach party town. This reputation has been somewhat tarnished in recent years by favela violence resulting from the narcotics trade and [militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_police_militias \"Brazilian police militias\").[[34]](#cite_note-37)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Tanques_ocupam_a_Avenida_Presidente_Vargas%2C_1968-04-04.jpg/250px-Tanques_ocupam_a_Avenida_Presidente_Vargas%2C_1968-04-04.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tanques_ocupam_a_Avenida_Presidente_Vargas,_1968-04-04.jpg)\n\nA convoy of [M41 Walker Bulldog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M41_Walker_Bulldog \"M41 Walker Bulldog\") tanks along the streets of the city in 1968 during the [military rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_military_government \"Brazilian military government\"). At the time, Rio de Janeiro was a city-state, capital of [Guanabara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_(state) \"Guanabara (state)\").\n\nPlans for moving the nation's capital city from Rio de Janeiro to the center of Brazil had been occasionally discussed, and when [Juscelino Kubitschek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juscelino_Kubitschek \"Juscelino Kubitschek\") was elected president in 1955, it was partially on the strength of promises to build a new capital.[[35]](#cite_note-38) Though many thought that it was just [campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_campaign \"Political campaign\") rhetoric, Kubitschek managed to have Brasília and a new [Federal District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distrito_Federal_(Brazil) \"Distrito Federal (Brazil)\") built, at great cost, by 1960. On 21 April of that year, the capital of Brazil was officially moved to Brasília. The territory of the former Federal District became its own state, Guanabara, after the bay that borders it to the east, encompassing just the city of Rio de Janeiro. After the [1964 coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Brazilian_coup_d'état \"1964 Brazilian coup d'état\") that installed a [military dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_military_government \"Brazilian military government\"), the [city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state \"City-state\") was the only state left in Brazil to oppose the military. Then, in 1975, a presidential decree known as \"The Fusion\" removed the city's federative status and merged it with the [State of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_(state) \"Rio de Janeiro (state)\"), with the city of Rio de Janeiro replacing [Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niterói \"Niterói\") as the state's capital, and establishing the [Rio de Janeiro Metropolitan Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Greater Rio de Janeiro\").[[36]](#cite_note-39)\n\nIn 1992, Rio hosted the [Earth Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_Summit \"Earth Summit\"), a [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations \"United Nations\") conference to fight [environmental degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_degradation \"Environmental degradation\"). Twenty years later, in 2012, the city hosted another conference on [sustainable development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_development \"Sustainable development\"), named [United Nations Conference on Sustainable Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Conference_on_Sustainable_Development \"United Nations Conference on Sustainable Development\"). The city hosted the [World Youth Day in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Youth_Day_2013 \"World Youth Day 2013\"), the second [World Youth Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Youth_Day \"World Youth Day\") in [South America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_America \"South America\") and first in Brazil. In the sports field, Rio de Janeiro was the host of the [2007 Pan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pan_American_Games \"2007 Pan American Games\") and the [2014 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_FIFA_World_Cup \"2014 FIFA World Cup\"). On 2 October 2009, the [International Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Olympic_Committee \"International Olympic Committee\") [announced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bids_for_the_2016_Summer_Olympics \"Bids for the 2016 Summer Olympics\") that Rio de Janeiro would host the [2016 Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics \"2016 Summer Olympics\") and the [2016 Paralympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Paralympics \"2016 Summer Paralympics\"), beating competitors [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago \"Chicago\"), [Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo \"Tokyo\"), and [Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid \"Madrid\"). The city became the first South American city to host the event and the second Latin American city (after [Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_City \"Mexico City\") in [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Summer_Olympics \"1968 Summer Olympics\")) to host the Games. Since the early 2010s, Rio de Janeiro has been inscribed as a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_World_Heritage_Site \"UNESCO World Heritage Site\") for its arts, urban culture and [designed landscapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Designed_landscape \"Designed landscape\") set around a natural environment.\n\n## Geography\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=7 \"Edit section: Geography\")]\n\nMain article: [Geography of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Geography of Rio de Janeiro\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/ISS-67_City_lights_of_Rio_De_Janeiro%2C_Brazil.jpg/250px-ISS-67_City_lights_of_Rio_De_Janeiro%2C_Brazil.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ISS-67_City_lights_of_Rio_De_Janeiro,_Brazil.jpg)\n\n[Satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite \"Satellite\") view of [Greater Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Greater Rio de Janeiro\") at night seen from [ISS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISS \"ISS\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/RiodeJaneiro_Municip_RiodeJaneiro.svg/250px-RiodeJaneiro_Municip_RiodeJaneiro.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RiodeJaneiro_Municip_RiodeJaneiro.svg)\n\nLocation in the state of Rio de Janeiro\n\nRio de Janeiro is near the west end of a strip (from [Cabo Frio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabo_Frio \"Cabo Frio\") to just east of [Ilha Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilha_Grande \"Ilha Grande\")) of Brazil's Atlantic coast close to the [Tropic of Capricorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Capricorn \"Tropic of Capricorn\") where the shoreline is oriented east and west; the city thus faces largely south. It was founded at the entrance to an inlet, [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay \"Guanabara Bay\") (Baía de Guanabara), which is marked by a point of land called Sugar Loaf (Pão de Açúcar) – a \"calling card\" of the city.[[37]](#cite_note-40)\n\nThe population of the city of Rio de Janeiro, occupying an area of 1,182.3 km2 (456.5 sq mi),[[38]](#cite_note-41) is about 6,000,000.[[39]](#cite_note-42) The population of the greater metropolitan area is estimated at 11–13.5 million. Residents of the city are known as *[cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca \"Carioca\")*. The official song of Rio is \"[Cidade Maravilhosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Maravilhosa \"Cidade Maravilhosa\")\", by composer [André Filho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/André_Filho \"André Filho\").\n\n### Parks\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=8 \"Edit section: Parks\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Parque_Lage_e_Corcovado.jpg/250px-Parque_Lage_e_Corcovado.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Parque_Lage_e_Corcovado.jpg)\n\n[Parque Lage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Lage \"Parque Lage\") with [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado \"Corcovado\") in the background\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Arcos_do_Jardim_Bot%C3%A2nico_do_Rio..jpg/250px-Arcos_do_Jardim_Bot%C3%A2nico_do_Rio..jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arcos_do_Jardim_Botnico_do_Rio..jpg)\n\nArches in the [Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Botanical_Garden \"Rio de Janeiro Botanical Garden\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/A_bela_vista_do_Cristo.jpg/250px-A_bela_vista_do_Cristo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:A_bela_vista_do_Cristo.jpg)\n\n[Rodrigo de Freitas Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_de_Freitas_Lagoon \"Rodrigo de Freitas Lagoon\")\n\nThe city has parks and ecological reserves such as the Tijuca National Park, the world's first urban forest and [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO \"UNESCO\") Environmental Heritage and Biosphere Reserve; [Pedra Branca State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedra_Branca_State_Park \"Pedra Branca State Park\"), which houses the highest point of Rio de Janeiro, the peak of Pedra Branca; the [Quinta da Boa Vista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinta_da_Boa_Vista \"Quinta da Boa Vista\") complex; the [Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botanical_Garden_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Botanical Garden of Rio de Janeiro\");[[40]](#cite_note-43) Rio's Zoo; [Parque Lage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Lage \"Parque Lage\"); and the [Passeio Público](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passeio_Público_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Passeio Público (Rio de Janeiro)\"), the first public park in the [Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas \"Americas\").[[41]](#cite_note-44) In addition the [Flamengo Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flamengo_Park \"Flamengo Park\") is the largest landfill in the city, extending from the center to the south zone, and containing museums and monuments, in addition to much vegetation.\n\nSince 1961, the [Tijuca National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca_Forest \"Tijuca Forest\") (Parque Nacional da Tijuca), the largest city-surrounded [urban forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_forest \"Urban forest\") and the second largest urban forest in the world, has been a National Park. The largest urban forest in the world is the Floresta da Pedra Branca (White Rock Forest), which is located in the West Zone of Rio de Janeiro.[[42]](#cite_note-45)\n\n### Environment\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=9 \"Edit section: Environment\")]\n\nDue to the high concentration of industries in the metropolitan region, the city has faced serious problems of environmental pollution. The [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay \"Guanabara Bay\") has lost mangrove areas and suffers from residues from domestic and industrial sewage, oils, and heavy metals. Although its waters renew when they reach the sea, the bay is the final receiver of all the tributaries generated along its banks and in the basins of the many rivers and streams that flow into it. The levels of particulate matter in the air are twice as high as those recommended by the [World Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Health_Organization \"World Health Organization\"), in part because of the large numbers of vehicles in circulation.[[43]](#cite_note-46)\n\nThe waters of Sepetiba Bay are slowly following the path traced by Guanabara Bay, with sewage generated by a population of the order of 1.29 million inhabitants being released without treatment in streams or rivers. With regard to industrial pollution, highly toxic wastes, with high concentrations of heavy metals – mainly [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc \"Zinc\") and [cadmium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadmium \"Cadmium\") – have been dumped over the years by factories in the industrial districts of [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Cruz, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Itaguaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itaguaí \"Itaguaí\") and [Nova Iguaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Iguaçu \"Nova Iguaçu\"), constructed under the supervision of State policies.[[44]](#cite_note-OQ-47)\n\nThe Marapendi lagoon and the [Rodrigo de Freitas Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_de_Freitas_Lagoon \"Rodrigo de Freitas Lagoon\") have suffered from the leniency of the authorities and the growth in the number of apartment buildings close by. The illegal discharge of sewage and the consequent deaths of algae diminished the oxygenation of the waters, causing fish mortality.[[45]](#cite_note-48)[[46]](#cite_note-49)\n\nThere are, on the other hand, signs of decontamination in the lagoon made through a [public-private partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership \"Public-private partnership\") established in 2008 to ensure that the lagoon waters will eventually be suitable for bathing. The decontamination actions involve the transfer of sludge to large craters present in the lagoon itself, and the creation of a new direct and underground connection with the sea, which will contribute to increasing the daily water exchange between the two environments. However, during the Olympics the lagoon hosted the rowing competitions, and there were numerous concerns about potential infection resulting from human sewage.[[47]](#cite_note-50)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/PanoramaRio.jpg/1920px-PanoramaRio.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:PanoramaRio.jpg \"Panorama of the city of Rio de Janeiro highlighting the mountains of Corcovado (left), Sugarloaf (center, background) and Two Brothers (right), from the Chinese Belvedere\")\n\nPanorama of the city of Rio de Janeiro highlighting the mountains of [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado \"Corcovado\") (left), [Sugarloaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain_(Brazil) \"Sugarloaf Mountain (Brazil)\") (center, background) and Two Brothers (right), from the [Chinese Belvedere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vista_Chinesa \"Vista Chinesa\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Ipanema_and_Leblon_as_seen_from_Morro_Dois_Irm%C3%A3os%2C_with_Lagoa_and_Cantagalo_in_the_background%2C_Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brazil.jpg/960px-Ipanema_and_Leblon_as_seen_from_Morro_Dois_Irm%C3%A3os%2C_with_Lagoa_and_Cantagalo_in_the_background%2C_Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brazil.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ipanema_and_Leblon_as_seen_from_Morro_Dois_Irmãos,_with_Lagoa_and_Cantagalo_in_the_background,_Rio_de_Janeiro,_Brazil.jpg \"Elevated view from Morro Dois Irmãos over Leblon, Ipanema and Lagoa Rodrigo de Freitas, with the forested hills beyond. Morro do Cantagalo and the Cantagalo–Pavão-Pavãozinho communities appear in the centre, while Ipanema Beach and Arpoador extend to the right. In the distance are Pão de Açúcar, the entrance to Guanabara Bay, and the coastal hills and islands of Niterói.\")\n\nElevated view from Morro Dois Irmãos over Leblon, Ipanema and Lagoa Rodrigo de Freitas, with the forested hills beyond. Morro do Cantagalo and the Cantagalo–Pavão-Pavãozinho communities appear in the centre, while Ipanema Beach and Arpoador extend to the right. In the distance are Pão de Açúcar, the entrance to Guanabara Bay, and the coastal hills and islands of Niterói.\n\n### Climate\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=10 \"Edit section: Climate\")]\n\n#### Overview\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=11 \"Edit section: Overview\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Tempestade_no_Rio_de_Janeiro_visto_do_P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7%C3%BAcar.jpg/250px-Tempestade_no_Rio_de_Janeiro_visto_do_P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7%C3%BAcar.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tempestade_no_Rio_de_Janeiro_visto_do_Pão_de_Açúcar.jpg)\n\nNight [storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storm \"Storm\") with [lightning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lightning \"Lightning\") in Rio de Janeiro, view from [Sugarloaf Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain \"Sugarloaf Mountain\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Praia_de_Copacabana_-_Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brasil.jpg/250px-Praia_de_Copacabana_-_Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brasil.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Praia_de_Copacabana_-_Rio_de_Janeiro,_Brasil.jpg)\n\n[Copacabana Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_beach \"Copacabana beach\") on a sunny day\n\nRio has a [tropical wet and dry climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_wet_and_dry_climate \"Tropical wet and dry climate\") ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Köppen_climate_classification \"Köppen climate classification\"): *Aw*, [Trewartha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trewartha_climate_classification \"Trewartha climate classification\"): *Awab* borderline *Awha*), and is often characterized by long periods of heavy rain between December and March.[[48]](#cite_note-51) The city experiences hot, humid summers, and warm, sunny winters. In inland areas of the city, temperatures above 40 °C (104 °F) are common during the summer, though rarely for long periods, while maximum temperatures above 27 °C (81 °F) occur regularly every month. Temperature varies according to elevation, distance from the coast, and type of vegetation or land use.\n\nSummers are hot and stormy. Temperatures over 40 °C (104 °F) may happen throughout the warmer half-year but are much more common during the summer, often meaning the actual \"feels-like\" temperature is over 50 °C (122 °F), when there is little wind and the [relative humidity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relative_humidity \"Relative humidity\") percentage is high.[[49]](#cite_note-52)[[50]](#cite_note-53)[[51]](#cite_note-54)[[52]](#cite_note-55) Along the coast, the breeze, blowing onshore and offshore, moderates the temperature. Heavy rain is common in the summer, and these have, on some occasions, provoked catastrophic floods and landslides. The mountainous areas register greater rainfall since they constitute a barrier to the humid wind that comes from the Atlantic.[[53]](#cite_note-56)\n\nWinters are warm and not as rainy. Cold fronts and dawn/morning sea breezes bring mild temperatures, though average temperatures remain above 25 °C (77 °F).\n\n#### Notable meteorological events\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=12 \"Edit section: Notable meteorological events\")]\n\nDrought is rare, albeit bound to happen occasionally given the city's strongly seasonal tropical climate. The Brazilian drought of 2014–2015, most severe in the [Southeast Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Region,_Brazil \"Southeast Region, Brazil\") and the worst in decades, affected the entire metropolitan region's water supply (a diversion from the [Paraíba do Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraíba_do_Sul \"Paraíba do Sul\") River to the [Guandu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guandu_River_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Guandu River (Rio de Janeiro)\") River is a major source for the state's most populous mesoregion). There were plans to divert the Paraíba do Sul to the [Sistema Cantareira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistema_Cantareira \"Sistema Cantareira\") (Cantareira system) during the water crisis of 2014 in order to help the critically drought-stricken [Greater São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_São_Paulo \"Greater São Paulo\") area. However, the availability of sufficient rainfall to supply tap water to both metropolitan areas in the future is merely speculative.[[54]](#cite_note-57)[[55]](#cite_note-58)[[56]](#cite_note-59)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Bondinho_do_P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7%C3%BAcar_by_Diego_Baravelli.jpg/250px-Bondinho_do_P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7%C3%BAcar_by_Diego_Baravelli.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bondinho_do_Pão_de_Açúcar_by_Diego_Baravelli.jpg)\n\n[Sugarloaf Cable Car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Cable_Car \"Sugarloaf Cable Car\") approaching the summit\n\nSome areas within [Rio de Janeiro state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_state \"Rio de Janeiro state\") occasionally have falls of [snow grains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_grains \"Snow grains\") and [ice pellets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_pellets \"Ice pellets\") (popularly called *granizo*) and [hail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail \"Hail\"),[[57]](#cite_note-60)[[58]](#cite_note-61)[[59]](#cite_note-62) and roughly in the same suburbs ([Nova Iguaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Iguaçu \"Nova Iguaçu\") and surrounding areas, including parts of Campo Grande and Bangu) that correspond to the location of the March 2012, February–March 2013 and January 2015 ice pellet (*granizo*) falls, there was a [tornado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado \"Tornado\")-like phenomenon in January 2011, for the first time in the region's recorded history, causing structural damage and long-lasting [blackouts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_outage \"Power outage\"), but no fatalities.[[60]](#cite_note-63)[[61]](#cite_note-9guaçutornado-64) The [World Meteorological Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Meteorological_Organization \"World Meteorological Organization\") has advised that Brazil, especially its southeastern region, must be prepared for increasingly [severe weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_weather \"Severe weather\") occurrences in the near future, since events such as the catastrophic [January 2011 Rio de Janeiro floods and mudslides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2011_Rio_de_Janeiro_floods_and_mudslides \"January 2011 Rio de Janeiro floods and mudslides\") are not an isolated phenomenon. In early May 2013, winds registering above 90 km/h (56 mph) caused blackouts in 15 neighborhoods of the city and three surrounding municipalities, and killed one person.[[62]](#cite_note-65) Rio saw similarly high winds (about 100 km/h (62 mph)) in January 2015.[[63]](#cite_note-66)\n\nAccording to data from the Brazilian National Institute of Meteorology (INMET), since 1931, the absolute minimum temperature recorded in Rio de Janeiro was 6.4 °C on 18 August 1933,[[64]](#cite_note-NCB-1931-1960-67) at the meteorological station in the [Bangu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangu,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Bangu, Rio de Janeiro\") neighborhood (deactivated in March 2004). This same station, located in the city's hottest neighborhood,[[65]](#cite_note-68) recorded a maximum temperature of 43.1 °C on 14 January 1984, which held the record for the highest temperature in the city until 26 December 2012, when 43.2 °C was recorded at the [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Cruz, Rio de Janeiro\") station.[[66]](#cite_note-69) The record for rainfall within 24 hours is 349.4 mm, recorded on 26 February 1971, at the former station in the [Engenho de Dentro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engenho_de_Dentro \"Engenho de Dentro\") neighborhood.[[67]](#cite_note-bdmep.inmet.gov.br-70)\n\n| Climate data for Rio de Janeiro ([Saúde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saúde,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Saúde, Rio de Janeiro\")), elevation
