Demographics\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=13 \"Edit section: Demographics\")]\n\nMain articles: [Brazilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilians \"Brazilians\"), [Cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariocas \"Cariocas\"), [Demographics of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Demographics of Rio de Janeiro\"), [Demographics of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Brazil \"Demographics of Brazil\"), and [Immigration to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Brazil \"Immigration to Brazil\")\n\nHistorical population\n\n| Year | Pop. | ±% |\n| 1872 | 274,972 | — |\n| 1890 | 522,651 | +90.1% |\n| 1900 | 811,443 | +55.3% |\n| 1920 | 1,157,873 | +42.7% |\n| 1940 | 1,764,141 | +52.4% |\n| 1950 | 2,377,451 | +34.8% |\n| 1960 | 3,281,908 | +38.0% |\n| 1970 | 4,251,918 | +29.6% |\n| 1980 | 5,090,700 | +19.7% |\n| 1991 | 5,336,179 | +4.8% |\n| 2000 | 5,851,914 | +9.7% |\n| 2010 | 6,320,446 | +8.0% |\n| 2022 | 6,211,223 | −1.7% |\n| [[89]](#cite_note-92) |\n\nAccording to the [2010 IBGE Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Geography_and_Statistics \"Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics\"), there were 5,940,224 people residing in the city of Rio de Janeiro.[[90]](#cite_note-93) Since 1960, when it was surpassed by [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo \"São Paulo\"), the city of Rio de Janeiro has been the second-most populous city in Brazil.[[91]](#cite_note-94)\n\n### Ethnic groups\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=14 \"Edit section: Ethnic groups\")]\n\nThe [2022 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_census \"2022 Brazilian census\") revealed the following numbers: [White Brazilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilian \"White Brazilian\") (45.4% or 2,821,619); [Mixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo_Brazilian \"Pardo Brazilian\") (38.7% or 2,403,895); [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilian \"Afro-Brazilian\") (15.6% or 968,428); [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Brazilian \"Asian Brazilian\") (10,514 or 0.2%); [Indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Brazilian \"Native Brazilian\") (6,531 or 0.1%).[[92]](#cite_note-95) The population of Rio de Janeiro was 53.2% [female](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female \"Female\") and 46.8% [male](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Male \"Male\").[[93]](#cite_note-autogenerated3-96)\n\nThe [black community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilian \"Afro-Brazilian\") was formed by residents whose ancestors had been trafficked into slavery, mostly from [Angola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angola \"Angola\") and [Mozambique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozambique \"Mozambique\"), as well as by people of Angolan, Mozambican and [West African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_African \"West African\") descent who moved to Rio from other parts of Brazil. Nearly half of the city's population is of mixed or black phenotype.[[94]](#cite_note-97) [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilian \"White Brazilian\") in Brazil is defined more by having a European-looking phenotype rather than ancestry, and two full [siblings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sibling \"Sibling\") can be of different \"racial\" categories[[95]](#cite_note-98) in a [skin color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_skin_color \"Human skin color\") and [phenotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype \"Phenotype\") continuum from *pálido* (*branco*) or fair-skinned, through *branco [moreno](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/moreno \"wikt:moreno\")* or swarthy Caucasian, *mestiço claro* or lighter skinned multiracial, *pardo* (mixed race) to *negro* or [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people \"Black people\"). [Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo \"Pardo\"), for example, in popular usage includes those who are *[caboclos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caboclo \"Caboclo\")* ([mestizos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizo \"Mestizo\")), *mulatos* ([mulattoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulattoes \"Mulattoes\")), *[cafuzos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cafuzo \"Cafuzo\")* ([zambos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambo \"Zambo\")), *juçaras* (archaic term for [tri-racials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial#Brazil \"Multiracial\")) and westernized [Amerindians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Brazil \"Indigenous peoples of Brazil\") (which are called *caboclos* as well), being more of a skin color rather than a racial group in particular.\n\n### Immigration and migration\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=15 \"Edit section: Immigration and migration\")]\n\nMain article: [Immigration to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Brazil \"Immigration to Brazil\")\n\nRace and ethnicity in Rio de Janeiro\n\n| Ethnicity | Percentage |\n| [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilians \"White Brazilians\") |  | 45.4% |\n| [Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo \"Pardo\") ([Multiracial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial#Brazil \"Multiracial\")) |  | 38.7% |\n| [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilians \"Afro-Brazilians\") |  | 15.6% |\n| [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Brazilian \"Asian Brazilian\") |  | 0.2% |\n| [Amerindian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Brazil \"Indigenous peoples in Brazil\") |  | 0.1% |\n\nDifferent ethnic groups contributed to the formation of the population of Rio de Janeiro. Before European colonization, there were at least seven different [indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Brazil \"Indigenous peoples in Brazil\") speaking 20 languages in the region. Some of them joined the Portuguese and the other the French. The Portuguese then exterminated those who joined the [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Brazilian \"French Brazilian\"), while the other part was assimilated.[[96]](#cite_note-99)\n\nRio de Janeiro is home to the largest [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian \"Portuguese Brazilian\") population outside of [Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon \"Lisbon\") in Portugal.[[97]](#cite_note-100) After independence from Portugal, Rio de Janeiro became a destination for hundreds of thousands of immigrants from Portugal, mainly in the early 20th century. The immigrants were mostly poor peasants who subsequently found prosperity in Rio as city workers and small traders.[[98]](#cite_note-101)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Afot3602.jpg/250px-Afot3602.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afot3602.jpg)\n\nPortuguese immigrant in Rio de Janeiro, considered the largest \"[Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian \"Portuguese Brazilian\") city\" outside Portugal[[99]](#cite_note-auto1-102)\n\nThe Portuguese cultural influence is still seen in many parts of the city (and many other parts of the state of Rio de Janeiro), including architecture and [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language \"Portuguese language\"). Most Brazilians with some cultural contact with Rio know how to easily differentiate between the local dialect, *Carioca* , and other Brazilian dialects. People of [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian \"Portuguese Brazilian\") ancestry predominate in most of the state. The Brazilian census of 1920 showed that 39.7% of the Portuguese who lived in Brazil lived in Rio de Janeiro. Including all of Rio de Janeiro, the proportion rose to 46.3% of the Portuguese who lived in Brazil. The numerical presence of the Portuguese was extremely high, accounting for 72% of the foreigners who lived in the capital. Portuguese-born people accounted for 20.4% of the population of Rio, and those with a Portuguese father or a Portuguese mother accounted for 30.8%. In other words, native born Portuguese and their children accounted for 51.2% of the inhabitants of Rio, or a total of 267,664 people in 1890.[[100]](#cite_note-103)\n\nRio de Janeiro city (1890)\n\n| Group | Population | Percentage[[101]](#cite_note-104) |\n| Portuguese immigrants | 106,461 | 20.4% |\n| Brazilians with at least one Portuguese parent | 161,203 | 30.8% |\n| Portuguese immigrants and their descendants | 267,664 | 51.2% |\n\nAs a result of the influx of immigrants to Brazil from the late 19th to the early 20th century, also found in Rio de Janeiro and [its metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Greater Rio de Janeiro\") are communities of [Levantine Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Brazilian \"Arab Brazilian\") who are mostly [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians \"Arab Christians\") or [Irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Brazil \"Irreligion in Brazil\"), [Spaniards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_immigration_to_Brazil \"Spanish immigration to Brazil\"), [Italians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Brazilian \"Italian Brazilian\"), [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Brazilian \"German Brazilian\"), [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Brazilians \"Japanese Brazilians\"),[[102]](#cite_note-105)\n\n| Genomic ancestry of non-related individuals in Rio de Janeiro[[103]](#cite_note-Suarez-Kurtz_Vargens_Self-reported_skin_color-106) |\n| --- |\n| Race or skin color | Number of individuals | Amerindian | African | European |\n| White | 107 | 6.7% | 6.9% | 86.4% |\n| *Pardo* (Mixed race) | 119 | 8.3% | 23.6% | 68.1% |\n| Black | 109 | 7.3% | 50.9% | 41.8% |\n\nAccording to an autosomal DNA study from 2009, conducted on a school in the poor suburb of Rio de Janeiro, the \"pardos\" there were found to be on average about 80% European, and the \"whites\" (who thought of themselves as \"very mixed\") were found to carry very little Amerindian and/or African admixtures. The results of the tests of genomic ancestry are quite different from the self-made estimates of European ancestry. In general, the test results showed that European ancestry is far more important than the students thought it would be. The \"pardos\" for example thought of themselves as 1⁄3 European, 1⁄3 African and 1⁄3 Amerindian before the tests, and yet their ancestry on average reached 80% European.[[104]](#cite_note-107) Other studies showed similar results[[103]](#cite_note-Suarez-Kurtz_Vargens_Self-reported_skin_color-106)[[105]](#cite_note-108)\n\n### Religion\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=16 \"Edit section: Religion\")]\n\nSee also: [Religion in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Brazil \"Religion in Brazil\"), [Protestantism in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Brazil \"Protestantism in Brazil\"), and [Roman Catholic Church in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church_in_Brazil \"Roman Catholic Church in Brazil\")\n\nReligion in Rio de Janeiro (2010 Census)[[106]](#cite_note-Religion-109)\n\n| Religion | Percent |\n| [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism \"Roman Catholicism\") |  | 51.1% |\n| [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism \"Protestantism\") |  | 23.4% |\n| Other Christian |  | 2.4% |\n| [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion \"Irreligion\") |  | 13.6% |\n| [Spiritism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kardecist_spiritism \"Kardecist spiritism\") |  | 5.9% |\n| Others |  | 3.6% |\n\nReligion in Rio de Janeiro is diverse, with [Catholic Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Christianity \"Catholic Christianity\") being the majority religion. According to data from the [Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Geography_and_Statistics \"Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics\") (IBGE), in 2010 the population of Rio de Janeiro had 3,229,192 [Roman Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Brazil \"Catholic Church in Brazil\") (51.1%), 1,477,021 [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Brazil \"Protestantism in Brazil\") (23.4%), 372.851 [Spiritists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kardecist_spiritism \"Kardecist spiritism\") (5.9%), 37,974 [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses \"Jehovah's Witnesses\") (0.6%), 75,075 [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Brazil \"Buddhism in Brazil\") (0.2%), 52,213 [Umbanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbanda \"Umbanda\") (0.8%), 21,800 [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism \"Judaism\") (0.3%), 25,743 [Brazilian Catholic Apostolic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Catholic_Apostolic_Church \"Brazilian Catholic Apostolic Church\") (0.4%), 16,776 new eastern religious (0.2%), 28,843 [Candomblé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candomblé \"Candomblé\") (0.4%), 3,853 [Mormons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormon \"Mormon\") (<0.1%), 5,751 [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox \"Eastern Orthodox\") Christians (<0.1%), 7,394 spiritualists (0.1%), 964 [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Brazil \"Islam in Brazil\") (<0.1%), 5,662 esoteric (<0.1%) and 802 [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu \"Hindu\") (<0.1%). 858,704 had no religion (13.5%), and 113,530 followed other forms of Christianity (1.8%).[[106]](#cite_note-Religion-109)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Aerial_view_of_the_Statue_of_Christ_the_Redeemer.jpg/250px-Aerial_view_of_the_Statue_of_Christ_the_Redeemer.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aerial_view_of_the_Statue_of_Christ_the_Redeemer.jpg)\n\n*[Christ the Redeemer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_the_Redeemer_(statue) \"Christ the Redeemer (statue)\")*\n\nRio de Janeiro has had a rich and influential Catholic tradition. The [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_São_Sebastião_do_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Roman Catholic Archdiocese of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro\") is the second largest archdiocese in Brazil after [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_São_Paulo \"Roman Catholic Archdiocese of São Paulo\").[[107]](#cite_note-110) The [Rio de Janeiro Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Cathedral \"Rio de Janeiro Cathedral\") was inaugurated in 1979, in the central region of the city. Its installations have a collection of great historical and religious value: the Archdiocesan Museum of Sacred Art and the Archdiocesan Archive.[[108]](#cite_note-Religion2-111) In a [Contemporary architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_architecture \"Contemporary architecture\"), it has a conical shape, with 96 meters of internal diameter and capacity to receive up to 20 thousand faithful. The splendor of the building, with straight and sober lines, is due to the changing stained glass windows carved on the walls up to the dome. Its design and execution was coordinated by [Monsignor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsignor \"Monsignor\") Ivo Antônio Calliari (1918–2005).[[108]](#cite_note-Religion2-111) [Saint Sebastian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Sebastian \"Saint Sebastian\") is recognized as the city's [patron saint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patron_saint \"Patron saint\"), which is why it received the canonical name of \"Saint Sebastian of Rio de Janeiro\".[[109]](#cite_note-112)\n\nMany [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant \"Protestant\") creeds coexist in the city, [Presbyterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian \"Presbyterian\"), [Congregational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_Churches \"Congregational Churches\"), [Lutheran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheran \"Lutheran\") and [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican \"Anglican\") Churches, in addition to evangelical churches such as the [Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist \"Baptist\"), [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist \"Methodist\"), [Seventh-day Adventist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist \"Seventh-day Adventist\"), and [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal \"Pentecostal\") churches, such as the [Universal Church of the Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Church_of_the_Kingdom_of_God \"Universal Church of the Kingdom of God\"), [Assembly of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_of_God \"Assembly of God\"), [Christian Congregation in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Congregation_in_Brazil \"Christian Congregation in Brazil\"), and [The Foursquare Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Foursquare_Church \"The Foursquare Church\").[[106]](#cite_note-Religion-109)\n\nAfro-Brazilian religions such as [Umbanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbanda \"Umbanda\") and [Candomblé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candomblé \"Candomblé\") find support in various social segments, although professed by less than 2% of the population, many Cariocas simultaneously observe those practices with Roman Catholicism.[[106]](#cite_note-Religion-109)[[110]](#cite_note-113)\n\n#### Christianity\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=17 \"Edit section: Christianity\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/2018_Rio_de_Janeiro_-_Igreja_Presbiteriana.jpg/250px-2018_Rio_de_Janeiro_-_Igreja_Presbiteriana.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2018_Rio_de_Janeiro_-_Igreja_Presbiteriana.jpg)\n\n[Presbyterian Cathedral of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_Cathedral_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Presbyterian Cathedral of Rio de Janeiro\")\n\nThe [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_São_Sebastião_do_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Roman Catholic Archdiocese of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro\"), the Metropolitan See of its respective [Ecclesiastical Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_Province \"Ecclesiastical Province\"), belongs to the Regional Episcopal Council Leste I of the [National Conference of Bishops of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Conference_of_Bishops_of_Brazil \"National Conference of Bishops of Brazil\") (CNBB) (headquartered in Rio until 1977). Founded in 1676, it covers a territory of 1,721 km2.[[111]](#cite_note-114)\n\nThe *[Cathedral of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_St._Sebastian_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Cathedral of St. Sebastian of Rio de Janeiro\")*, or *Metropolitan Cathedral*, was inaugurated in 1979 in the  [central region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Centro, Rio de Janeiro\") of the city. Its facilities house a collection of great historical and religious value: the Archdiocesan Museum of Sacred Art and the Archdiocesan Archive. The Banco da Providência and the Archdiocesan [Caritas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caritas_Brazil \"Caritas Brazil\") are also based there. In a contemporary architectural style, it has a conical shape, with an internal diameter of 96 meters and a capacity to hold up to 20,000 worshippers. The splendor of the building, with its straight and sober lines, is due to the changing [stained glass windows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stained_glass_windows \"Stained glass windows\") carved into the walls up to the [dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome \"Dome\"). Its design and execution were coordinated by [Monsignor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsignor \"Monsignor\") [Ivo Antônio Calliari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Antônio_Calliari?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Ivo Antônio Calliari (page does not exist)\") (1918–2005).[[112]](#cite_note-115)\n\nThe city is home to various [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism \"Protestantism\") or reformed denominations, exemplified by the [Presbyterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_of_Brazil \"Presbyterian Church of Brazil\"), [Congregational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_church \"Congregational church\"), [Lutheran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheran \"Lutheran\"), and [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican \"Anglican\") churches. There are also evangelical churches such as the [Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist \"Baptist\"), [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist \"Methodist\"), [Seventh-day Adventist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist \"Seventh-day Adventist\") churches, and those of [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism \"Pentecostalism\") origin: [Universal Church of the Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Church_of_the_Kingdom_of_God \"Universal Church of the Kingdom of God\"), [Assembly of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblies_of_God \"Assemblies of God\"), [Christian Congregation in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Congregation_in_Brazil \"Christian Congregation in Brazil\"), [Foursquare Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foursquare_Church \"Foursquare Church\"), [House of Blessing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabernacle_Evangelical_Church_of_Jesus?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Tabernacle Evangelical Church of Jesus (page does not exist)\"), [God is Love Pentecostal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_is_Love_Pentecostal_Church \"God is Love Pentecostal Church\"), [Christian Maranatha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maranatha_Christian_Church?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Maranatha Christian Church (page does not exist)\"), and New Life.[[113]](#cite_note-sidra1-116)\n\n### Education\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=18 \"Edit section: Education\")]\n\nMain article: [List of Rio de Janeiro schools, colleges, universities and research centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Rio_de_Janeiro_schools,_colleges,_universities_and_research_centers \"List of Rio de Janeiro schools, colleges, universities and research centers\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Centro_ci%C3%AAncias_humanas.JPG/250px-Centro_ci%C3%AAncias_humanas.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Centro_ciências_humanas.JPG)\n\nCenter for [Human Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_science \"Human science\") of the [Federal University of the State of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_the_State_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Federal University of the State of Rio de Janeiro\")\n\nThe [Portuguese language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language \"Portuguese language\") is the official and [national language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_language \"National language\"), and thus the primary language taught in schools. [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language \"English language\") and [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language \"Spanish language\") are also part of the official curriculum. There are also international schools, such as the [American School of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_School_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"American School of Rio de Janeiro\"), Our Lady of Mercy School, SIS Swiss International School, the Corcovado German School, the Lycée Français and the British School of Rio de Janeiro.[[114]](#cite_note-117)\n\nThe city has several universities and research institutes. The [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_(Brazil) \"Ministry of Education (Brazil)\") has certified approximately 99 upper-learning institutions in Rio.[[115]](#cite_note-118) The most prestigious university is the [Federal University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Federal University of Rio de Janeiro\"). It is the fifth-best in Latin America; the second-best in Brazil, second only to the [University of São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_São_Paulo \"University of São Paulo\"); and the best in [Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_America \"Latin America\"), according to the [QS World University Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings \"QS World University Rankings\").[[116]](#cite_note-119)[[117]](#cite_note-120)\n\nSome notable higher education institutions are [Federal University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Federal University of Rio de Janeiro\") (UFRJ); [Federal University of the State of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidade_Federal_do_Estado_do_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Universidade Federal do Estado do Rio de Janeiro\") (UNIRIO); [Rio de Janeiro State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_University \"Rio de Janeiro State University\") (UERJ); [Federal Rural University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidade_Federal_Rural_do_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Universidade Federal Rural do Rio de Janeiro\") (UFRRJ, often nicknamed *Rural*); [Fluminense Federal University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluminense_Federal_University \"Fluminense Federal University\") (UFF); [Pontifical Catholic University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontifical_Catholic_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Pontifical Catholic University of Rio de Janeiro\") (PUC-Rio); [Getúlio Vargas Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundação_Getúlio_Vargas \"Fundação Getúlio Vargas\") (FGV); [Military Institute of Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Militar_de_Engenharia \"Instituto Militar de Engenharia\") (IME); [Superior Institute of Technology in Computer Science of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Superior_de_Tecnologia_em_Ciências_da_Computação_do_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Instituto Superior de Tecnologia em Ciências da Computação do Rio de Janeiro\") (IST-Rio); College of Publicity and Marketing (ESPM); [The Brazilian Center for Research in Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Brasileiro_de_Pesquisas_Físicas \"Centro Brasileiro de Pesquisas Físicas\") (CBPF); [National Institute of Pure and Applied Mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_de_Matemática_Pura_e_Aplicada \"Instituto Nacional de Matemática Pura e Aplicada\") (IMPA); Superior institute of Education of Rio de Janeiro (ISERJ) and [Federal Center of Technological Education Celso Suckow da Fonseca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Federal_de_Educação_Tecnológica_Celso_Suckow_da_Fonseca \"Centro Federal de Educação Tecnológica Celso Suckow da Fonseca\") (CEFET/RJ). There are more than 137 upper-learning institutions in whole Rio de Janeiro state.[[118]](#cite_note-121)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Patioiserj.jpg/250px-Patioiserj.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Patioiserj.jpg)\n\nSuperior institute of Education of Rio de Janeiro (ISERJ)\n\nThe [Rio de Janeiro State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_University \"Rio de Janeiro State University\") (public), [Federal University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Federal University of Rio de Janeiro\") (public), Brazilian Institute of Capital Markets (private) and [Pontifical Catholic University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontifical_Catholic_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Pontifical Catholic University of Rio de Janeiro\") (private) are among the country's top institutions of higher education. Other institutes of higher learning include the Colégio Regina Coeli in Usina, notable for having its own [3 ft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3_ft_gauge_railways \"3 ft gauge railways\") (914 mm) [narrow-gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow-gauge_railway \"Narrow-gauge railway\")[[119]](#cite_note-122) [funicular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funicular \"Funicular\") railway on its grounds.[[120]](#cite_note-123)\n\nPrimary schools are largely under municipal administration, while the state plays a more significant role in the extensive network of secondary schools. There are also a small number of schools under federal administration, such as Pedro II School, Colégio de Aplicação da UFRJ, and the Centro Federal de Educação Tecnológica of Rio de Janeiro (CEFET-RJ). In addition, Rio has an ample offering of private schools that provide education at all levels. Rio is home to many colleges and universities. The literacy rate for [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca \"Carioca\") aged 10 and older is nearly 95 percent, well above the national average.[[121]](#cite_note-124) In Rio, there were 1,033 primary schools with 25,594 teachers and 667,788 students in 1995. There are 370 secondary schools with 9,699 teachers and 227,892 students. There are 53 University-preparatory schools with 14,864 teachers and 154,447 students. The city has six major universities and 47 private schools of higher learning.[[122]](#cite_note-125)\n\n### Social issues\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=19 \"Edit section: Social issues\")]\n\nMain articles: [Social issues in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_issues_in_Brazil \"Social issues in Brazil\"), [Social apartheid in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_apartheid_in_Brazil \"Social apartheid in Brazil\"), and [Favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela \"Favela\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/1_rocinha_aerial_pano_2014.jpg/250px-1_rocinha_aerial_pano_2014.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1_rocinha_aerial_pano_2014.jpg)\n\n[Rocinha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocinha \"Rocinha\"), the largest [slum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slum \"Slum\") (*[favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela \"Favela\")*) in Brazil\n\nThere are significant disparities between the rich and the poor in Rio de Janeiro, and different socioeconomic groups are largely segregated into different neighborhoods.[[123]](#cite_note-126) Although the city clearly ranks among the world's major metropolises, large numbers live in slums known as [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela \"Favela\"), where 95% of the population are poor, compared to 40% in the general population.[[124]](#cite_note-127)\n\nThere have been a number of government initiatives to counter this problem, from the removal of the population from favelas to [housing projects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_housing \"Public housing\") such as [Cidade de Deus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_de_Deus_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Cidade de Deus (Rio de Janeiro)\") to the more recent approach of improving conditions in the favelas and bringing them up to par with the rest of the city, as was the focus of the \"Favela Bairro\" program and deployment of [Pacifying Police Units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifying_Police_Unit \"Pacifying Police Unit\").\n\nRio has the second highest population of favela residents in Brazil, but the 11th highest proportion of its total population living in favelas.[[125]](#cite_note-128) More than 1.3 million people live in its 813 favelas, 21% of Rio's total population.[[126]](#cite_note-129)\n\nRio also has a large proportion of state-sanctioned violence, with about 20% of all killings committed by state security.[[127]](#cite_note-130) In 2019, police killed an average of five people each day in the state of Rio de Janeiro, with a total of 1,810 killed in the year. This was more police killings than any year since official records started in 1998.[[128]](#cite_note-131)\n\n## Politics\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=20 \"Edit section: Politics\")]\n\n### Municipal government\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=21 \"Edit section: Municipal government\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Centro_Administrativo_Municipal_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o_%282025-05-03%29.jpg/250px-Centro_Administrativo_Municipal_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o_%282025-05-03%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Centro_Administrativo_Municipal_São_Sebastião_(2025-05-03).jpg)\n\nSão Sebastião Administrative Center, seat of City Hall\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/PedroErnestoPalace1-CCBY.jpg/250px-PedroErnestoPalace1-CCBY.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:PedroErnestoPalace1-CCBY.jpg)\n\n[Pedro Ernesto Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Ernesto_Palace \"Pedro Ernesto Palace\"), in [Cinelndia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinelndia \"Cinelndia\"), seat of [Municipal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_Chamber_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Municipal Chamber of Rio de Janeiro\")\n\nIn Rio de Janeiro, the executive power is represented by the mayor and the cabinet of secretaries, in accordance with the model proposed by the Federal Constitution. The Organic Law of the Municipality and the current Master Plan, however, stipulate that the public administration must provide the population with effective tools for the exercise of participatory democracy. In this way, the city is divided into subprefectures, each of which is headed by a sub-mayor appointed directly by the mayor.[[129]](#cite_note-132)\n\nLegislative power is constituted by the municipal council, composed of 51 councilors[[130]](#cite_note-133) elected for four-year terms (in compliance with the provisions of article 29 of the Constitution, which disciplines a minimum number of 42 and a maximum of 55 for municipalities with more than five million inhabitants).[[131]](#cite_note-134) It is up to the House to prepare and vote on fundamental laws for the administration and the Executive, especially the participatory budget (Lei de Diretrizes Orçamentárias). Although the veto power is granted to the mayor, the process of voting on the laws that oppose him usually generates conflicts between the Executive and the Legislative.[[132]](#cite_note-135)\n\nThere are also municipal councils, which complement the legislative process and the work engendered in the secretariats. Compulsorily formed by representatives of various sectors of organized civil society, they are on different fronts — although their effective representation is sometimes questioned. The following are currently in operation: Municipal Council for the Protection of Cultural Heritage (CMPC), Defense of the Environment (CONDEMAM), Health (CMS), the Rights of Children and Adolescents (CMDCA), Education (CME), Social Assistance (CMAS) and Anti-Drugs.[[133]](#cite_note-136)\n\n### State government\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=22 \"Edit section: State government\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Pal%C3%A1cio_Guanabara_em_agosto_de_2017.jpg/250px-Pal%C3%A1cio_Guanabara_em_agosto_de_2017.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Palácio_Guanabara_em_agosto_de_2017.jpg)\n\n[Guanabara Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Palace \"Guanabara Palace\"), seat of the state government\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Pal%C3%A1cio_Tiradentes.jpg/250px-Pal%C3%A1cio_Tiradentes.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Palácio_Tiradentes.jpg)\n\n[Tiradentes Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiradentes_Palace \"Tiradentes Palace\"), seat of the [Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Legislative Assembly of Rio de Janeiro\")\n\nAs the capital of the homonymous state, the city is the seat of the state government. The [Guanabara Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Palace \"Guanabara Palace\") (formerly known as Paço Isabel) is located in the Laranjeiras neighborhood, in the south zone, and is the official seat of the Rio de Janeiro executive power. Not to be confused with Palácio Laranjeiras, situated in the same neighborhood, which is the official residence of the governor of Rio de Janeiro.[[134]](#cite_note-hist-137)\n\nThe [Legislative Assembly of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Legislative Assembly of Rio de Janeiro\") (ALERJ) is the state legislative body[[135]](#cite_note-138) and is headquartered at [Tiradentes Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiradentes_Palace \"Tiradentes Palace\"),[[136]](#cite_note-139) where the [Chamber of Deputies of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_Brazil \"Chamber of Deputies of Brazil\") previously functioned.[[137]](#cite_note-Acervo_O_Globo-140)\n\nThe Court of Justice of the State of Rio de Janeiro (TJRJ) is the highest body of the judiciary in the state. Its central court is located in downtown Rio de Janeiro, but, from 2013 to July 2018, some of the courts of this court were moved to Cidade Nova.[[138]](#cite_note-141)\n\n### Federal government\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=23 \"Edit section: Federal government\")]\n\nThe city of Rio de Janeiro was successively the capital of the Portuguese colony of the [State of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Brazil \"State of Brazil\") (1621–1815), after the [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves \"United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves\") (1815–1822), the [Empire of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil \"Empire of Brazil\") (1822–1889) and from the Republic of the United States of Brazil (1889–1968) until 1960, when the seat of government was definitively transferred to the then newly built [Brasília](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brasília \"Brasília\").[[139]](#cite_note-nexo-142)\n\nDespite the change in the federal capital, 59% of civil servants in the Executive Branch of federal agencies and public companies remained in the city. Rio de Janeiro is also the only Brazilian state where the number of federal employees exceeds the number of state employees. About a third of all federal public bodies and companies remain in the former capital, with 50 public offices, including agencies, autarchies, foundations and public companies, such as the [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil \"National Library of Brazil\"), the [National Nuclear Energy Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Nuclear_Energy_Commission \"National Nuclear Energy Commission\"), [Fiocruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiocruz \"Fiocruz\"), [BNDES](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNDES \"BNDES\"), [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras \"Petrobras\"), [Eletrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletrobras \"Eletrobras\"), [IBGE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBGE \"IBGE\"), [Casa da Moeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_da_Moeda_do_Brasil \"Casa da Moeda do Brasil\"), the [National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_National_Archives \"Brazilian National Archives\"), among others.[[139]](#cite_note-nexo-142)\n\n### Subdivisions\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=24 \"Edit section: Subdivisions\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/Zoneamento_da_Cidade_do_Rio_de_Janeiro.svg/500px-Zoneamento_da_Cidade_do_Rio_de_Janeiro.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zoneamento_da_Cidade_do_Rio_de_Janeiro.svg)\n\nMunicipality of Rio de Janeiro and its division into zones and neighborhoods\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| West Zone | North Zone | South Zone | Central Zone |\n\nThe city is commonly divided into the historic center (Centro); the tourist-friendly wealthier South Zone (Zona Sul); the residential less wealthy North Zone (Zona Norte); peripheries in the West Zone (Zona Oeste), among them [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Cruz, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Campo Grande, Rio de Janeiro\") and the wealthy newer [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\") district. Rio de Janeiro is administratively divided into 33 *distritos* (districts) named *Regiões Administrativas* (\"Administrative Regions\") and 166 *bairros* (neighborhoods).[[140]](#cite_note-143)\n\nSubprefectures are officially grouped into four regions (or \"zones\"), taking into account geographic position and occupation history; however, these do not have any administrative or political power over the municipality. The official political division of the municipality takes into account historical-cultural characteristics to divide the neighborhoods.[[141]](#cite_note-:0-144) Most of its population is concentrated in the neighborhoods of Campo Grande, Santa Cruz, Bangu, Tijuca, Realengo, Jacarepaguá, Copacabana, Barra da Tijuca, Maré, Guaratiba and Taquara together, these eleven neighborhoods concentrate a population of 1.5 million inhabitants, according to the 2010 census.[[142]](#cite_note-145)\n\n[*Centro* or Downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Zona Central, Rio de Janeiro\") is the historic core of the city, as well as its financial and commercial center. Sites of interest include the [Paço Imperial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paço_Imperial \"Paço Imperial\"), built during colonial times to serve as a residence for the Portuguese governors of Brazil; many historic churches, such as the [Candelária Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candelária_Church \"Candelária Church\") (the former cathedral), São José, Santa Lucia, Nossa Senhora do Carmo, Santa Rita, São Francisco de Paula, and the monasteries of Santo Antônio and São Bento. The Centro also houses the modern concrete [Rio de Janeiro Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Cathedral \"Rio de Janeiro Cathedral\"). Around the [Cinelndia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinelndia \"Cinelndia\") square, there are several landmarks of the [*Belle Époque* of Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Belle_Époque \"Brazilian Belle Époque\"), such as the [Municipal Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Municipal_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Teatro Municipal (Rio de Janeiro)\") and the [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil \"National Library of Brazil\") building. Among its several museums, the [Museu Nacional de Belas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes \"Museu Nacional de Belas Artes\") (National Museum of Fine Arts) and the [Museu Histórico Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historical_Museum_(Brazil) \"National Historical Museum (Brazil)\") (National Historical Museum) are the most important.\n\nThe Cultural Corridor in Rio's City Center is home to one of the most architecturally and historically preserved districts, known as the Sociedade de Amigos das Adjacências da Rua da Alfndega (SAARA). This association was formed by merchants operating near Rua da Alfndega in the Historic Center of Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. The area is renowned for its stores specializing in popular home goods and fabrics, housed within several historic buildings. The name comes from the occupation of the region by Muslim, Jewish, and Maronite Christian immigrants from the Ottoman Empire in the early 20th century, for commercial purposes. The region preserves examples of various architectural styles, from Portuguese colonial, through Neoclassical, Eclectic, Art Deco, and Modernism, making the region a rich tapestry of historical architecture that refers to the city's colonial and imperial past.\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Forte_de_Copacabana_panorama.jpg/250px-Forte_de_Copacabana_panorama.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Forte_de_Copacabana_panorama.jpg)\n\nAerial view of [Fort Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Copacabana \"Fort Copacabana\"), with [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Copacabana (Rio de Janeiro)\") (right) and [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema \"Ipanema\") (left, background)\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Praia_da_Barra_e_Montanhas_do_Parque_Nacional_da_Tijuca.jpg/250px-Praia_da_Barra_e_Montanhas_do_Parque_Nacional_da_Tijuca.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Praia_da_Barra_e_Montanhas_do_Parque_Nacional_da_Tijuca.jpg)\n\n[Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\") with [Pedra da Gávea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedra_da_Gávea \"Pedra da Gávea\") in background\n\nThe [South Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Zone_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"South Zone (Rio de Janeiro)\") (*Zona Sul*) is composed of several districts, among which are [São Conrado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Conrado \"São Conrado\"), [Leblon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leblon \"Leblon\"), [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema \"Ipanema\"), [Arpoador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arpoador \"Arpoador\"), [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Copacabana (Rio de Janeiro)\"), and [Leme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leme_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Leme (Rio de Janeiro)\"), which compose Rio's Atlantic beach coastline.[[143]](#cite_note-146) The neighborhood of Copacabana beach hosts one of the world's most spectacular New Year's Eve parties (\"Reveillon\"), as more than two million revelers crowd onto the sands to watch the [fireworks display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fireworks \"Fireworks\"). From 2001, the fireworks have been launched from boats, to improve the safety of the event.[[144]](#cite_note-147)\n\nThe North Zone (*Zona Norte*) begins at Grande Tijuca (the middle class residential and commercial *bairro* of [Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca \"Tijuca\")), just west of the city center, and sprawls for miles inland until [Baixada Fluminense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baixada_Fluminense \"Baixada Fluminense\") and the city's Northwest.[[145]](#cite_note-148) This region is home to the Maracanã (located in Grande Tijuca), once the world's highest capacity [football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_football \"Association football\") venue, able to hold nearly 199,854 people,[[146]](#cite_note-149) as it did for the [World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_World_Cup \"FIFA World Cup\") [final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguay_v_Brazil_(1950_FIFA_World_Cup) \"Uruguay v Brazil (1950 FIFA World Cup)\") of [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_FIFA_World_Cup \"1950 FIFA World Cup\"). This region is also home to most of the [samba schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba_school \"Samba school\") of Rio de Janeiro, such as Mangueira, Salgueiro, Império Serrano, Unidos da Tijuca, Imperatriz Leopoldinense, among others. Some of the main neighborhoods of Rio's North Zone are Alto da Boa Vista, which shares the [Tijuca Rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca_Forest \"Tijuca Forest\") with the South and Southwest Zones; Tijuca, Vila Isabel, Méier, [São Cristovão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Cristóvão_(Rio_de_Janeiro_neighborhood) \"São Cristóvão (Rio de Janeiro neighborhood)\"), Madureira, Penha, Manguinhos, Fundão, Olaria, among others. Many of Rio de Janeiro's [slums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slums \"Slums\") (*[favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas \"Favelas\")*) are located in the North Zone.[[147]](#cite_note-150)\n\nWest Zone (*Zona Oeste*) of Rio de Janeiro is a vaguely defined area that covers some 50% of the city's entire area, including Barra da Tijuca and Recreio dos Bandeirantes neighborhoods. The West Side of Rio has many historic sites because of the old \"Royal Road of Santa Cruz\" that crossed the territory in the regions of Realengo, Bangu, and Campo Grande, finishing at the Royal Palace of Santa Cruz in the Santa Cruz region. The highest peak of the city of Rio de Janeiro is the Pedra Branca Peak (Pico da Pedra Branca) inside the [Pedra Branca State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedra_Branca_State_Park \"Pedra Branca State Park\"). It has an altitude of 1024m. The Pedra Branca State Park (Parque Estadual da Pedra Branca)[[148]](#cite_note-151) is the biggest urban state park in the world comprising 17 neighborhoods in the west side, being a \"giant lung\" in the city with trails,[[149]](#cite_note-152) waterfalls and historic constructions like an old aqueduct in the Colônia Juliano Moreira[[150]](#cite_note-153) in the neighborhood of [Taquara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taquara,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Taquara, Rio de Janeiro\") and a dam in [Camorim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camorim \"Camorim\"). Santa Cruz and Campo Grande Region have exhibited economic growth, mainly in the [Campo Grande neighborhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Campo Grande, Rio de Janeiro\"). Industrial enterprises are being built in lower and lower middle class residential [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Cruz, Rio de Janeiro\"), one of the largest and most populous of Rio de Janeiro's neighborhoods, most notably [Ternium Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternium \"Ternium\"), a new steel mill with its own private docks on Sepetiba Bay, which is planned to be South America's largest steel works.[[151]](#cite_note-154) A tunnel called *Túnel da Grota Funda*, opened in 2012, creating a [public transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transit \"Public transit\") facility between Barra da Tijuca and Santa Cruz, lessening travel time to the region from other areas of Rio de Janeiro.[[152]](#cite_note-VejaRio2011-155)\n\nOn 9 September 2025, many [neighbourhoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighbourhood \"Neighbourhood\") that comprise the former West Zone were set apart to create the Southwest Zone (*Zona Sudoeste*). The Southwest Zone is compound by [Anil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anil,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Anil, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\"), [Camorim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camorim \"Camorim\"), [Cidade de Deus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_de_Deus,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Cidade de Deus, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Curicica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curicica \"Curicica\"), [Freguesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freguesia_(Jacarepaguá) \"Freguesia (Jacarepaguá)\"), [Gardênia Azul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gardênia_Azul \"Gardênia Azul\"), [Grumari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumari \"Grumari\"), [Itanhangá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itanhangá,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Itanhangá, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepaguá \"Jacarepaguá\"), [Joá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joá \"Joá\"), [Praça Seca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça_Seca \"Praça Seca\"), [Pechincha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechincha \"Pechincha\"), [Rio das Pedras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_das_Pedras,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Rio das Pedras, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Recreio dos Bandeirantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recreio_dos_Bandeirantes \"Recreio dos Bandeirantes\"), [Tanque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanque \"Tanque\"), [Taquara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taquara,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Taquara, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Vargem Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vargem_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Vargem Grande, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Vargem Pequena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vargem_Pequena \"Vargem Pequena\") and [Vila Valqueire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vila_Valqueire \"Vila Valqueire\").[[153]](#cite_note-156)\n\n## International relations\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=25 \"Edit section: International relations\")]\n\nSee also: [List of twin towns and sister cities in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_twin_towns_and_sister_cities_in_Brazil \"List of twin towns and sister cities in Brazil\")\n\n### Twin towns – sister cities\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=26 \"Edit section: Twin towns – sister cities\")]\n\nRio de Janeiro is [twinned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twin_towns_and_sister_cities \"Twin towns and sister cities\") with:\n\n* ![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Flag_of_Portugal_%28official%29.svg/40px-Flag_of_Portugal_%28official%29.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) [Arganil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arganil \"Arganil\"), Portugal[[154]](#cite_note-157)\n* ![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/40px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta \"Atlanta\"), United States[[155]](#cite_note-158)\n* ![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Flag_of_Azerbaijan.svg/40px-Flag_of_Azerbaijan.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) [Baku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baku \"Baku\"), Azerbaijan[[156]](#cite_note-riointernacional-159)\n* Cities\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=28 \"Edit section: Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities\")]\n\nRio de Janeiro is a part of the [Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Ibero-American_Capital_Cities \"Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities\").[[197]](#cite_note-200)\n\n## Economy\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=29 \"Edit section: Economy\")]\n\nMain articles: [Economy of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Economy of Rio de Janeiro\") and [Economy of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Brazil \"Economy of Brazil\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Urca%2C_Rio_de_Janeiro_-_State_of_Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brazil_-_panoramio_%2814%29.jpg/250px-Urca%2C_Rio_de_Janeiro_-_State_of_Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brazil_-_panoramio_%2814%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Urca,_Rio_de_Janeiro_-_State_of_Rio_de_Janeiro,_Brazil_-_panoramio_(14).jpg)\n\n[Downtown Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Centro (Rio de Janeiro)\"), in the financial district of the city\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7ucar_-_Sugarloaf_Mountain_-_Zuckerhut_-_Botafago.jpg/250px-P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7ucar_-_Sugarloaf_Mountain_-_Zuckerhut_-_Botafago.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pão_de_Açucar_-_Sugarloaf_Mountain_-_Zuckerhut_-_Botafago.jpg)\n\n[Botafogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo \"Botafogo\") with the [Sugarloaf Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain_(Brazil) \"Sugarloaf Mountain (Brazil)\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Convento_de_Santo_Ant%C3%B4nio_do_Largo_da_Carioca_19.jpg/250px-Convento_de_Santo_Ant%C3%B4nio_do_Largo_da_Carioca_19.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Convento_de_Santo_Antônio_do_Largo_da_Carioca_19.jpg)\n\nLargo da Carioca, in Downtown Rio\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Barra_da_Tijuca_-_panoramio.jpg/250px-Barra_da_Tijuca_-_panoramio.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Barra_da_Tijuca_-_panoramio.jpg)\n\n[Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\")\n\nRio de Janeiro has the second-largest [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_domestic_product \"Gross domestic product\") of any city in Brazil, surpassed only by [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo \"São Paulo\"). According to the [IBGE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Geography_and_Statistics \"Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics\"), it was approximately US$201 billion in 2008, equivalent to 5.1% of the national total. Taking into consideration the network of influence exerted by the urban [metropolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolis \"Metropolis\") (which covers 11.3% of the population), this share in GDP rises to 14.4%, according to a study released in October 2008 by the IBGE.[[198]](#cite_note-201)\n\nGreater Rio de Janeiro, as perceived by the IBGE, has a GDP of US$187 billion, constituting the second largest hub of national wealth. Per capita GDP is US$11,786.[[199]](#cite_note-202) It concentrates 68% of the state's economic strength and 7.9% of all goods and services produced in the country.[[200]](#cite_note-203) The services sector comprises the largest portion of GDP (65.5%), followed by commerce (23.4%), industrial activities (11.1%) and agriculture (0.1%).[[201]](#cite_note-204)[[202]](#cite_note-205)\n\nBenefiting from the federal capital position it had for a long period (1763–1960), the city became a dynamic administrative, financial, commercial, and cultural center. Rio de Janeiro became an attractive place for companies to locate when it was the capital of Brazil, as important sectors of society and of the government were present in the city. The city was chosen as headquarters for [state-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_property \"Public property\") companies such as [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras \"Petrobras\"), [Eletrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletrobras \"Eletrobras\"), [Caixa Econômica Federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caixa_Econômica_Federal \"Caixa Econômica Federal\"), [National Economic and Social Development Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Development_Bank \"Brazilian Development Bank\") and [Vale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vale_(mining_company) \"Vale (mining company)\") (which was privatized in the 1990s). The [Rio de Janeiro Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Stock_Exchange \"Rio de Janeiro Stock Exchange\") (BVRJ), which currently trades only government securities, was the first stock exchange founded in Brazil in 1845.\n\nThe offshore oil exploration in the [Campos Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campos_Basin \"Campos Basin\") began in 1968 and became the main site for oil production in Brazil. This caused many oil and gas companies to be based in Rio de Janeiro, such as the Brazilian branches of [Shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Dutch_Shell \"Royal Dutch Shell\"), [EBX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EBX_Group \"EBX Group\") and [Esso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esso \"Esso\"). For many years Rio was the second largest industrial hub of Brazil,[[203]](#cite_note-206) with [oil refineries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_refineries \"Oil refineries\"), shipbuilding industries, steel, [metallurgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallurgy \"Metallurgy\"), petrochemicals, cement, [pharmaceutical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutic \"Pharmaceutic\"), textile, processed foods and furniture industries.\n\nMajor international pharmaceutical companies have their Brazilian headquarters in Rio, such as: [Merck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merck_KGaA \"Merck KGaA\"), [Roche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche_Applied_Science \"Roche Applied Science\"), Arrow, Darrow, [Baxter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baxter_International \"Baxter International\"), Mayne, and Mappel.\n\nRecent decades have seen a sharp transformation in its economic profile, which is becoming more and more one of a major national hub of services and businesses.[[204]](#cite_note-207) The city is the headquarters of large telecom companies, such as [Intelig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelig_Telecom \"Intelig Telecom\"), [Oi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oi_(telecommunications) \"Oi (telecommunications)\") and [Embratel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embratel \"Embratel\"). Major Brazilian entertainment and media organizations are based in Rio de Janeiro, such as [Organizações Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organizações_Globo \"Organizações Globo\"), and also some of Brazil's major newspapers: *[Jornal do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jornal_do_Brasil \"Jornal do Brasil\")*, *[O Dia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Dia \"O Dia\")*, and *Business Rio*.\n\nTourism and entertainment are other key aspects of the city's economic life. The city is the nation's top tourist attraction for both Brazilians and foreigners.[[205]](#cite_note-208)\n\nIn Greater Rio, which has one of the highest per capita incomes in Brazil, retail trade is substantial. Many of the most important retail stores are located in the center. Still, others are scattered throughout the commercial areas of the other districts, where shopping centers, supermarkets, and other retail businesses handle a large volume of consumer trade.[[206]](#cite_note-209)\n\nRio de Janeiro is (as of 2014[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit)) the second largest exporting municipality in Brazil. Annually, Rio exported a total of US$7.49B worth of goods.[[207]](#cite_note-210) The top three goods exported by the municipality were [crude petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum \"Petroleum\") (40%), [semi-finished iron products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-finished_casting_products \"Semi-finished casting products\") (16%), and semi-finished steel products (11%).[[208]](#cite_note-211) Material categories of mineral products (42%) and metals (29%) make up 71% of all exports from Rio.[[209]](#cite_note-212)\n\nCompared to other cities, Rio de Janeiro's economy is the second-largest in Brazil, behind [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo \"São Paulo\"), and the [30th largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP \"List of cities by GDP\") with a GDP of [R$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Real \"Brazilian Real\") 201,9 billion in 2010. The per capita income for the city was R$22,903 in 2007 (around US$14,630).[[210]](#cite_note-213) Largely because of the strength of Brazil's currency at the time, [Mercer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_(consulting_firm) \"Mercer (consulting firm)\")'s [city rankings of cost of living for expatriate employees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_expensive_cities_for_expatriate_employees#Mercer_surveys \"List of most expensive cities for expatriate employees\"), reported that Rio de Janeiro ranked 12th among the most expensive cities in the world in 2011, up from the 29th position in 2010, just behind São Paulo (ranked 10th), and ahead of London, Paris, [Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milan \"Milan\"), and New York.[[211]](#cite_note-214)[[212]](#cite_note-215) Rio also had the most expensive hotel rates in Brazil, and the daily rate of its [five star hotels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_star_hotel \"Five star hotel\") were the second most expensive in the world after only New York.[[213]](#cite_note-216)\n\n### Highlighted sectors\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=30 \"Edit section: Highlighted sectors\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Edise.jpg/250px-Edise.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Edise.jpg)\n\nHeadquarters building of [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras \"Petrobras\")\n\n|  |\n| --- |\n| This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(August 2024)* |\n\nRio de Janeiro is home to many of Brazil's largest business conglomerates. Among them are the three largest multinationals in the energy and mining sectors: [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras \"Petrobras\"), [Vale S.A.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vale_S.A. \"Vale S.A.\"), and the [EBX Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EBX_Group \"EBX Group\"); the largest media and communications group in Latin America, [Grupo Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupo_Globo \"Grupo Globo\"); and major telecommunications companies like [CorpCo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CorpCo?action=edit&redlink=1 \"CorpCo (page does not exist)\") (owner of [Oi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oi_(company)?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Oi (company) (page does not exist)\") and [Portugal Telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_Telecom \"Portugal Telecom\")), [TIM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TIM_Brasil \"TIM Brasil\"), [Embratel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embratel \"Embratel\"), [Intelig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelig?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Intelig (page does not exist)\"), and [Star One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embratel_Star_One \"Embratel Star One\") (the largest satellite management company in Latin America).[[214]](#cite_note-217)\n\nIn the petrochemical sector, there are more than 700 companies, including Brazil's largest ([Shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shell_plc \"Shell plc\"), [Esso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esso \"Esso\"), [Ipiranga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipiranga_(company) \"Ipiranga (company)\"), [Chevron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_Corporation \"Chevron Corporation\"), [PRIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PRIO_S.A. \"PRIO S.A.\"), [Repsol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repsol \"Repsol\")). Most maintain research centers throughout the state and together produce over four-fifths of the petroleum and fuels distributed at service stations nationwide.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*] The [Companhia Siderúrgica Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Companhia_Siderúrgica_Nacional \"Companhia Siderúrgica Nacional\") (CSN),[[215]](#cite_note-218) [Ternium Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternium_Brasil?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Ternium Brasil (page does not exist)\")[[216]](#cite_note-219) (the largest steel mill in Latin America[[217]](#cite_note-220)) and the Brazilian branch of [BHP Billiton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BHP \"BHP\")[[218]](#cite_note-221) play significant roles in the mining sector. The city also hosts the main national and international groups of the [shipbuilding industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipbuilding_industry \"Shipbuilding industry\") and the largest shipyards in the state and throughout Brazil, which produce about 90% of the ships and [offshore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_construction \"Offshore construction\") equipment in Brazil.[[219]](#cite_note-222)\n\n[Americanas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americanas \"Americanas\"), [Coca-Cola Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca-Cola_Brasil?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Coca-Cola Brasil (page does not exist)\"), [Eletrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletrobras \"Eletrobras\"), [Michelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin \"Michelin\"), [Neoenergia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoenergia?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Neoenergia (page does not exist)\"), [Xerox do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerox \"Xerox\"), GE Oil & Gas, [Light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_S/A?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Light S/A (page does not exist)\"), [Chemtech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemtech?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Chemtech (page does not exist)\"), [Transpetro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transpetro \"Transpetro\"), [BAT Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAT_Brasil?action=edit&redlink=1 \"BAT Brasil (page does not exist)\"), [Grupo SulAmérica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SulAmérica_Seguros \"SulAmérica Seguros\") and [Vibra Energia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vibra_Energia \"Vibra Energia\") are among the major companies headquartered in the city. The city has a significant number of pharmaceutical industries, including [Schering-Plough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schering-Plough \"Schering-Plough\"),[[220]](#cite_note-223) [GlaxoSmithKline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GlaxoSmithKline \"GlaxoSmithKline\"),[[221]](#cite_note-224) [Roche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoffmann–La_Roche \"Hoffmann–La Roche\"),[[222]](#cite_note-225) and [Merck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merck_KGaA \"Merck KGaA\").[[223]](#cite_note-226)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Projac%2C_Globo.jpg/250px-Projac%2C_Globo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Projac,_Globo.jpg)\n\nReplica of the [Ganges River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_River \"Ganges River\") at the scenic city of [Projac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Projac \"Projac\"), the second-largest television complex in [Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_America \"Latin America\")[[224]](#cite_note-227)\n\nRio de Janeiro has inherited a strong cultural vocation from its past. It currently hosts the main production centers of Brazilian television: the [Estúdios Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estúdios_Globo \"Estúdios Globo\") of [TV Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Globo \"TV Globo\"), the [Casablanca Estúdios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casablanca_Estúdios?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Casablanca Estúdios (page does not exist)\") of [Record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Record_(TV_network) \"Record (TV network)\"), and the *Polo de Cinema de Jacarepaguá* — responsible for creating about 10,000 direct jobs and 30,000 indirect jobs. In 2006, 65% of Brazilian cinema production was done exclusively by studios in Rio, generating 91 million reais in federal funds through [tax incentive laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rouanet_Law \"Rouanet Law\").[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*] A significant part of the Brazilian editorial graphic industry is also present. In the phonographic industry, companies such as [EMI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EMI \"EMI\"),[[225]](#cite_note-228) [Universal Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Music_Group \"Universal Music Group\"),[[226]](#cite_note-229) [Sony Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sony_Music \"Sony Music\"),[[227]](#cite_note-230) [Warner Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warner_Music_Brasil \"Warner Music Brasil\")[[228]](#cite_note-231) and [Som Livre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Som_Livre \"Som Livre\") are present.[[229]](#cite_note-232)\n\nMany state-owned companies, public foundations, and federal autarchies have their headquarters in the city, including the [National Bank for Economic and Social Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bank_for_Economic_and_Social_Development \"National Bank for Economic and Social Development\") (BNDES),[[230]](#cite_note-233) the [Casa da Moeda do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_da_Moeda_do_Brasil \"Casa da Moeda do Brasil\"),[[231]](#cite_note-234) the [Indústrias Nucleares do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indústrias_Nucleares_do_Brasil?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Indústrias Nucleares do Brasil (page does not exist)\") (INB),[[232]](#cite_note-235) the [Financiadora de Estudos e Projetos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financiadora_de_Estudos_e_Projetos \"Financiadora de Estudos e Projetos\") (FINEP),[[233]](#cite_note-236) the [Instituto Brasileiro de Geografia e Estatística](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Brasileiro_de_Geografia_e_Estatística \"Instituto Brasileiro de Geografia e Estatística\") (IBGE),[[234]](#cite_note-237) the [Instituto Nacional de Metrologia, Normalização e Qualidade Industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_de_Metrologia,_Qualidade_e_Tecnologia \"Instituto Nacional de Metrologia, Qualidade e Tecnologia\") (Inmetro),[[235]](#cite_note-238) the [Instituto Nacional da Propriedade Industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_da_Propriedade_Industrial_(Brasil)?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Instituto Nacional da Propriedade Industrial (Brasil) (page does not exist)\") (INPI),[[236]](#cite_note-239) the [Comissão de Valores Mobiliários](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comissão_de_Valores_Mobiliários \"Comissão de Valores Mobiliários\") (CVM),[[237]](#cite_note-240) and the [Comissão Nacional de Energia Nuclear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comissão_Nacional_de_Energia_Nuclear \"Comissão Nacional de Energia Nuclear\") (CNEN).[[238]](#cite_note-241)\n\n### Tourism\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=31 \"Edit section: Tourism\")]\n\nMain article: [Tourism in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Brazil \"Tourism in Brazil\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Ipanema_Paradise.jpg/250px-Ipanema_Paradise.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ipanema_Paradise.jpg)\n\n[Ipanema beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema_beach \"Ipanema beach\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Museu_do_Amanh%C3%A3_-_entrada_%2828044443423%29.jpg/250px-Museu_do_Amanh%C3%A3_-_entrada_%2828044443423%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Museu_do_Amanhã_-_entrada_(28044443423).jpg)\n\n[Museum of Tomorrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Tomorrow \"Museum of Tomorrow\")\n\nRio de Janeiro is Brazil's primary tourist attraction and resort. It receives the most visitors per year of any city in South America, with 2.82 million international tourists a year.[[239]](#cite_note-242)\n\nAttractions in the city include approximately 80 kilometers of beaches, [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado \"Corcovado\") and [Sugarloaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain_(Brazil) \"Sugarloaf Mountain (Brazil)\") mountains, and the [Maracanã Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracanã_Stadium \"Maracanã Stadium\"). While the city had in the past had a thriving tourism sector, the industry entered a decline in the last quarter of the 20th century. Annual international airport arrivals dropped from 621,000 to 378,000, and average hotel occupancy dropped to 50% between 1985 and 1993.[[240]](#cite_note-243) The fact that [Brasília](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brasília \"Brasília\") replaced Rio de Janeiro as the Brazilian capital in 1960 and that [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo \"São Paulo\") replaced Rio as the country's commercial, financial and main cultural center during the mid-20th century, has also been cited as a leading cause of the decline.[[241]](#cite_note-Worldwide_destinations-244)\n\nRio de Janeiro's government has since undertaken to modernize the city's economy, reduce its chronic social inequalities, and improve its commercial standing as part of an initiative for the regeneration of the tourism industry.[[241]](#cite_note-Worldwide_destinations-244)\n\nRio de Janeiro is an international hub of active and diverse [nightlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightlife \"Nightlife\") with [bars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_(establishment) \"Bar (establishment)\"), dance bars and [nightclubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightclub \"Nightclub\") staying open well past midnight.[[242]](#cite_note-245) The city is a global [LGBTQ destination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_tourism \"LGBTQ tourism\"), with 1 million LGBT tourists visiting each year.[[243]](#cite_note-246)\n\nFarme de Amoedo Street (*Rua Farme de Amoedo*) is located in [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema \"Ipanema\"). The street and the nearby beach are popular in the [LGBTQ community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_community \"LGBTQ community\").[[244]](#cite_note-247)[[245]](#cite_note-248)\n\n## Infrastructure\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=32 \"Edit section: Infrastructure\")]\n\n### Public transportation\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=33 \"Edit section: Public transportation\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Public_transport_map_of_Rio_de_Janeiro.svg/500px-Public_transport_map_of_Rio_de_Janeiro.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Public_transport_map_of_Rio_de_Janeiro.svg)\n\nPublic transport map of Rio de Janeiro\n\nRio de Janeiro's [public transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transportation \"Public transportation\") modes include [city buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_bus \"Transit bus\"), [coach buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coach_(bus) \"Coach (bus)\"), [BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_Rapid_Transit \"Bus Rapid Transit\"), [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit \"Rapid transit\"), [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_rail \"Suburban rail\"), [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail \"Light rail\"), [ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries \"Ferries\"), [inclined elevators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclined_elevator \"Inclined elevator\"), [vans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_taxi \"Share taxi\") and a [cable car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondola_lift \"Gondola lift\"). Collectively, they carry over 2.6 million passengers per day.[[246]](#cite_note-General_public_transport_passenger_numbers_within_municipality-249) The high [fares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fares \"Fares\") on Rio's public transportation have significantly contributed to the high levels of [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_inequality \"Social inequality\") and [economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality \"Economic inequality\") inequality in the city[[247]](#cite_note-250) and were the main motive of the [2013 protest movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_protests_in_Brazil \"2013 protests in Brazil\").[[248]](#cite_note-251) Although there is limited fare integration through the [RioCard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RioCard \"RioCard\"), Rio de Janeiro's fare systems are still disjointed and not fully accessible.[[249]](#cite_note-252)\n\nIn 2022, the average amount of time people spent commuting with public transit in Rio de Janeiro on a weekday was 67 minutes. 12% of public transit riders had commutes that took more than two hours per day. The average amount of time people waited at a stop or station for public transit was 21 minutes, with 12% of riders waiting less than 5 minutes and 41% of riders waiting for over 20 minutes. The average distance people usually traveled in a single trip with public transit was 11.4 km (7.1 mi).
