The average distance people walked on their commute was 634 m (0.39 miles). 11% of people made exactly two transfers during their commute, and 2% of commuters made 3 or more transfers.[[250]](#cite_note-253)\n\n#### Buses and vans\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=34 \"Edit section: Buses and vans\")]\n\nMain article: [Bus transport in Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_transport_in_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Bus transport in Rio de Janeiro\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/Rio_de_Janeiro_city_bus.jpg/250px-Rio_de_Janeiro_city_bus.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rio_de_Janeiro_city_bus.jpg)\n\n[City bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_bus \"Transit bus\") in mixed traffic\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Apache_Vip_5_da_Mobi-Rio_parado_no_Terminal_Aroldo_Melodia_em_Cidade_Universit%C3%A1ria%2C_bairro_do_Rio_de_Janeiro.jpg/250px-Apache_Vip_5_da_Mobi-Rio_parado_no_Terminal_Aroldo_Melodia_em_Cidade_Universit%C3%A1ria%2C_bairro_do_Rio_de_Janeiro.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Apache_Vip_5_da_Mobi-Rio_parado_no_Terminal_Aroldo_Melodia_em_Cidade_Universitária,_bairro_do_Rio_de_Janeiro.jpg)\n\n[Transcarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcarioca \"Transcarioca\") Bus Rapid Transit (BRT)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Bairro_de_Anchieta.JPG/250px-Bairro_de_Anchieta.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bairro_de_Anchieta.JPG)\n\n[Van](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_taxi \"Share taxi\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/2015-01-21_-_Evanil.JPG/250px-2015-01-21_-_Evanil.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2015-01-21_-_Evanil.JPG)\n\n[Coach bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coach_bus \"Coach bus\") used for local intracity service\n\nAs of 2023, there are 354 municipal bus lines serving over 1.75 million passengers per day, as well as intercity lines.[[251]](#cite_note-Bus_fleet_numbers_within_municipality-254) The city has 20 [bus lanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_lanes \"Bus lanes\").[[252]](#cite_note-255) Most conventional bus routes are operated by 28 private companies divided into 4 [consortiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consortiums \"Consortiums\"), though 3 routes are run by the city government.[[253]](#cite_note-256)[[254]](#cite_note-MOBI-Rio-257) Although most buses are [conventional city buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_bus \"Transit bus\"), there are also a handful of [coach buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coach_bus \"Coach bus\") used for local intracity service with more expensive fares.[[255]](#cite_note-258)[[256]](#cite_note-259)[[257]](#cite_note-260)[[258]](#cite_note-261)[[259]](#cite_note-262)\n\nThe [Bus Rapid Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_Rapid_Transit \"Bus Rapid Transit\") system consists of 31 lines running along 4 corridors with [a wide variety of service patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-stop \"Limited-stop\"): [Transbrasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransBrasil_(bus_rapid_transit) \"TransBrasil (bus rapid transit)\"), [Transcarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcarioca \"Transcarioca\"), [Transolímpica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transolímpica \"Transolímpica\") and [Transoeste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transoeste \"Transoeste\"). The municipal government runs the system through Mobi-Rio, which is a [state-owned enterprise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprise \"State-owned enterprise\").[[254]](#cite_note-MOBI-Rio-257) It is 157 km (98 mi) long and has 153 stations as well as 731 buses that carry 535,000 passengers per day.[[260]](#cite_note-263)[[261]](#cite_note-264)\n\nIn addition to buses, Rio de Janeiro also has an extensive system of [vans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_taxi \"Share taxi\") used for public transport. This mode can more effectively traverse the steep, narrow and winding roads common in many [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas \"Favelas\") than buses and is often a lifeline for them.[[262]](#cite_note-265)[[263]](#cite_note-266)[[264]](#cite_note-267) However, most vans are run by [criminal organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_crime \"Organized crime\"), particularly [police militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_police_militias \"Brazilian police militias\"), which use their fares as a major source of revenue: Of the approximately 10,000 vans used for public transport in the city, only 2,000 are legal. The 2,000 legal vans carry approximately 310,000 passengers per day.[[265]](#cite_note-268)[[266]](#cite_note-269)[[267]](#cite_note-270)\n\nRio de Janeiro also has [intercity, interstate and international bus lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercity_bus_service \"Intercity bus service\"). Most long-distance bus lines run to [Novo Rio Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo_Rio_Bus_Terminal \"Novo Rio Bus Terminal\") at the edge of the [Zona Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Zona Central, Rio de Janeiro\").[[268]](#cite_note-271)[[269]](#cite_note-272) Long-distance buses also run to [bus stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_stations \"Bus stations\") in [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Campo Grande, Rio de Janeiro\")[[270]](#cite_note-273) and [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\").[[271]](#cite_note-274)\n\n#### Urban rail\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=35 \"Edit section: Urban rail\")]\n\nMain articles: [Rio de Janeiro Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Metro \"Rio de Janeiro Metro\"), [SuperVia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SuperVia \"SuperVia\"), [Rio de Janeiro Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Light_Rail \"Rio de Janeiro Light Rail\"), and [Santa Teresa Tram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Teresa_Tram \"Santa Teresa Tram\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Metro_Rio_01_2013_Ipanema_Osorio_5376.JPG/250px-Metro_Rio_01_2013_Ipanema_Osorio_5376.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Metro_Rio_01_2013_Ipanema_Osorio_5376.JPG)\n\nTrain of Rio de Janeiro Metro\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Esta%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Corte_8_-_Trem_Serie_3000_%282%29.jpg/250px-Esta%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Corte_8_-_Trem_Serie_3000_%282%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Estação_Corte_8_-_Trem_Serie_3000_(2).jpg)\n\nUrban train from SuperVia\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/VLT_na_Pra%C3%A7a_Mau%C3%A1_02.jpg/250px-VLT_na_Pra%C3%A7a_Mau%C3%A1_02.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:VLT_na_Praça_Mauá_02.jpg)\n\nRio de Janeiro Light Rail\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Rio_de_Janeiro_tram_06_at_Largo_do_Guimar%C3%A3es.jpg/250px-Rio_de_Janeiro_tram_06_at_Largo_do_Guimar%C3%A3es.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rio_de_Janeiro_tram_06_at_Largo_do_Guimarães.jpg)\n\nSanta Teresa Tram\n\nRio de Janeiro nominally has three [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit \"Rapid transit\") lines, though two of them (Lines [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_1_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Line 1 (Rio de Janeiro)\") and [4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_4_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Line 4 (Rio de Janeiro)\")) are operationally run as a single line.[[272]](#cite_note-275) The system runs on 58 km (36 mi) of track and has 41 stations.[[273]](#cite_note-276) Line 1/4 runs from [Uruguai/Tijuca station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguai_Station \"Uruguai Station\") in [Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca \"Tijuca\") to [Jardim Ocenico/Barra da Tijuca station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jardim_Ocenico_Station \"Jardim Ocenico Station\") in [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\"); [Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_2_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Line 2 (Rio de Janeiro)\") runs from [Pavuna station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavuna_Station \"Pavuna Station\") in [Pavuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavuna \"Pavuna\") to [Botafogo station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo_Station \"Botafogo Station\") in [Botafogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo \"Botafogo\"). Line 1/4 and Line 2 share tracks between [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil \"Central do Brasil\") and Botafogo. The metro system carries 651,000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passengers \"Passengers\") per day.[[274]](#cite_note-277)\n\nThe city also has a [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_rail \"Suburban rail\") system operated by [SuperVia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SuperVia \"SuperVia\") that connects the city of Rio with other locations in [Greater Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Greater Rio de Janeiro\"). It has 8 lines on 270 km (168 mi) of track with 103 stations in 12 [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_municipalities \"Brazilian municipalities\"); Rio de Janeiro municipality itself has 127 km (79 mi) of track with 59 stations on 5 lines. The [Deodoro Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deodoro_Line \"Deodoro Line\") runs from [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil \"Central do Brasil\") to [Deodoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deodoro,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Deodoro, Rio de Janeiro\") via [Maracanã](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracanã,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Maracanã, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Méier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Méier \"Méier\") and [Madureira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madureira,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Madureira, Rio de Janeiro\"); the [Santa Cruz Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Line \"Santa Cruz Line\") runs from Central do Brasil to [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Cruz, Rio de Janeiro\"), running parallel to the Deodoro line for its entire length and then via [Realengo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realengo \"Realengo\"), [Bangu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangu,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Bangu, Rio de Janeiro\") and [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Campo Grande, Rio de Janeiro\"); the [Japeri Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japeri_Line \"Japeri Line\") shares tracks with the Santa Cruz Line between Central do Brasil and Deodoro before continuing on to [Japeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japeri \"Japeri\") via [Anchieta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchieta,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Anchieta, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Nilópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilópolis \"Nilópolis\"), [Mesquita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesquita,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Mesquita, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Nova Iguaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Iguaçu \"Nova Iguaçu\") and [Queimados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queimados \"Queimados\"); the [Belford Roxo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belford_Roxo_Line?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Belford Roxo Line (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linha%20Belford%20Roxo \"pt:Linha Belford Roxo\")] and [Saracuruna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saracuruna_Line?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Saracuruna Line (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linha%20Saracuruna \"pt:Linha Saracuruna\")] Lines run parallel to the Deodoro, Santa Cruz and Japeri Lines between Central do Brasil and Maracanã, with the Belford Roxo Line continuing on to [Belford Roxo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belford_Roxo \"Belford Roxo\") via Madureira, [Pavuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavuna \"Pavuna\") and [São João de Meriti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_João_de_Meriti \"São João de Meriti\") and the Saracuruna Line continuing on to [Duque de Caxias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duque_de_Caxias,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Duque de Caxias, Rio de Janeiro\") via [Bonsucesso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonsucesso \"Bonsucesso\"), [Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramos,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Ramos, Rio de Janeiro\") and [Penha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penha,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Penha, Rio de Janeiro\").[[275]](#cite_note-278)[[276]](#cite_note-279)[[277]](#cite_note-280) The suburban rail system carries around 342,000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger \"Passenger\") per day.[[278]](#cite_note-281)\n\nThe Rio de Janeiro Light Rail is a modern [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail \"Light rail\") system that runs entirely within the [Zona Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Zona Central, Rio de Janeiro\") on 28 km (17 mi) of track with 30 stations distributed along 4 heavily interlined lines: Line 1 runs from [Santos Dumont Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos_Dumont_Airport \"Santos Dumont Airport\") to [Gentileza Intermodal Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentileza_Intermodal_Terminal \"Gentileza Intermodal Terminal\") via [Carioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Station \"Carioca Station\"), the [Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Port of Rio de Janeiro\") and [Novo Rio Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo_Rio_Bus_Terminal \"Novo Rio Bus Terminal\"); Line 2 runs from [Praça XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça_XV \"Praça XV\") to [Praia Formosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praia_Formosa,_Rio_de_Janeiro?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Praia Formosa, Rio de Janeiro (page does not exist)\") via [Praça Tiradentes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça_Tiradentes?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Praça Tiradentes (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça%20Tiradentes%20(Rio%20de%20Janeiro) \"pt:Praça Tiradentes (Rio de Janeiro)\")], [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil \"Central do Brasil\") and Novo Rio Bus Terminal; Line 3 runs from Santos Dumont Airport to Central do Brasil via Carioca and [Pequena África](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pequena_África?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Pequena África (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pequena%20África \"pt:Pequena África\")]; Line 4 shares track with Line 2 for almost its entire route, but stops at Gentileza Intermodal Terminal instead of Praia Formosa.[[279]](#cite_note-282)[[280]](#cite_note-283) The trams are the first in the world to use a combination of ground-level power supply (APS) and on-board supercapacitor energy storage (SRS), in order to eliminate overhead lines along the entire route.[[281]](#cite_note-284)[[282]](#cite_note-taut-2016july-285) The network uses 32 [Alstom Citadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alstom_Citadis \"Alstom Citadis\") 402 [low-floor trams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-floor_tram \"Low-floor tram\") carrying 420 passengers each. They are [bi-directional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bi-directional_vehicle \"Bi-directional vehicle\"), [air-conditioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_conditioning \"Air conditioning\"), have seven sections, and eight doors per side.[[283]](#cite_note-286)[[284]](#cite_note-RGI-2016jun6-287) The light rail system carries 101,000 passengers per day.[[285]](#cite_note-Transbrasil/Light_Rail_passenger_numbers-288)\n\nThe Santa Teresa Tram runs from [Carioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Station \"Carioca Station\") to [Santa Teresa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Teresa,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Teresa, Rio de Janeiro\") and is the oldest operating electric [tramway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tram \"Tram\") in [Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_America \"Latin America\"),[[286]](#cite_note-morrison-TLA-289) having commenced electric operation in 1896, replacing [horse-drawn trams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horsecar \"Horsecar\") and extending the route.[[287]](#cite_note-morrison-brazil-290) At this time the gauge was altered to 1,100 mm (3 ft 7+5⁄16 in) so that the trams would fit on top of [Carioca Aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Aqueduct \"Carioca Aqueduct\").[[287]](#cite_note-morrison-brazil-290)[[288]](#cite_note-291) It was designated a national historic monument in 1985.[[287]](#cite_note-morrison-brazil-290): 111 After a [derailment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derailment \"Derailment\") occurred on 27 August 2011,[[289]](#cite_note-292) which left six dead; tram service was suspended to improve the system. The elderly tramcars, which dated from the 1950s,[[290]](#cite_note-morrison-vehicles-293) were retired and replaced with newly built replicas that have the appearance of the old fleet but with new mechanical equipment and additional safety features;[[291]](#cite_note-taut-2013may-294) delivery began in 2014.[[292]](#cite_note-ODia29Sep2014-295) The line's track was also rebuilt, and after some delays, about one-third of the line reopened in July 2015. More sections reopened later in stages, following repair of additional sections of track. The main section of the line was restored to its full pre-2011 length of 6 km (4 mi) in January 2019.[[293]](#cite_note-taut-2020apr-296) The 1.7 km (1.1 mi) Paula Mattos branch to Largo das Neves, which was also closed in 2011, was reopened in January 2025.[[294]](#cite_note-297) Santa Teresa residents can ride the tram for free, but ticket prices are prohibitively expensive for other [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca \"Carioca\") and, because of this, the line is primarily used by tourists.[[295]](#cite_note-298) The tram gets an average of 1900 passengers per day.[[296]](#cite_note-299)\n\n#### Ferries\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=36 \"Edit section: Ferries\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Barca_P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7%C3%BAcar_%282%29.jpg/250px-Barca_P%C3%A3o_de_A%C3%A7%C3%BAcar_%282%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Barca_Pão_de_Açúcar_(2).jpg)\n\nRio de Janeiro ferry\n\nRio de Janeiro has [ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferry \"Ferry\") routes running radially out from [Praça XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça_Quinze_de_Novembro \"Praça Quinze de Novembro\") in the [city center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Centro, Rio de Janeiro\") to [Praça Araribóia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça_Araribóia?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Praça Araribóia (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça%20Araribóia \"pt:Praça Araribóia\")] (in [Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niterói \"Niterói\")'s city center), [Charitas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charitas \"Charitas\") (in the South side of Niterói), [Cocotá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocotá \"Cocotá\") (on [Governador Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governador_Island \"Governador Island\")) and [Paquetá Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paquetá_Island \"Paquetá Island\").[[297]](#cite_note-300) All four ferry routes run entirely through [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay \"Guanabara Bay\").[[298]](#cite_note-301) The Praça XV-Praça Araribóia route is by far the busiest and carries [commuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commuters \"Commuters\") from Niterói, [São Gonçalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Gonçalo,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"São Gonçalo, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Itaboraí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itaboraí \"Itaboraí\") and [Maricá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maricá \"Maricá\").[[299]](#cite_note-302)[[300]](#cite_note-303) Each ferry carries up to 2000 passengers,[[301]](#cite_note-304) with the ferry system as a whole carrying 35,000 passengers per day.[[302]](#cite_note-305)\n\n#### Cable car\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=37 \"Edit section: Cable car\")]\n\nThe [Providência Cable Car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teleférico_da_Providência \"Teleférico da Providência\") is a 721 m (0.45 miles) long [cable car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondola_lift \"Gondola lift\") line that runs from [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil \"Central do Brasil\") to [Gamboa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamboa,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Gamboa, Rio de Janeiro\") via [Providência Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morro_da_Providência \"Morro da Providência\"), serving the city's oldest [favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela \"Favela\").[[303]](#cite_note-306) The cable car first opened in 2014, was abandoned in 2017 and reopened in 2024.[[304]](#cite_note-307)[[305]](#cite_note-308) The line uses 16 gondolas and carries 9000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passengers \"Passengers\") per day.[[306]](#cite_note-309)[[307]](#cite_note-310)\n\n#### Inclined elevators\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=38 \"Edit section: Inclined elevators\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Plano_Inclinado_do_Pav%C3%A3o-Pav%C3%A3ozinho.jpg/250px-Plano_Inclinado_do_Pav%C3%A3o-Pav%C3%A3ozinho.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Plano_Inclinado_do_Pavão-Pavãozinho.jpg)\n\nPavão-Pavãozinho Inclined Elevator\n\nRio de Janeiro has 8 public [inclined elevators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclined_elevator \"Inclined elevator\") climbing 5 [hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hills \"Hills\"), all of which are run by the [municipal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_municipalities \"Brazilian municipalities\") through the Municipal Energy and Illumination Company (RioLuz).[[308]](#cite_note-Penna_Inclined_Elevator_Reform-311)[[309]](#cite_note-312) The Santa Marta Inclined Elevator runs up [Dona Marta Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela_Santa_Marta \"Favela Santa Marta\")[[310]](#cite_note-313) in two sections with 5 total stations[[311]](#cite_note-314) and carries 5000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passengers \"Passengers\") per day.[[312]](#cite_note-315) The Pavão-Pavãozinho Inclined Elevator runs from [General Osório/Ipanema station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Osório_Station \"General Osório Station\") to [Cantagalo-Pavão-Pavãozinho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantagalo-Pavão-Pavãozinho \"Cantagalo-Pavão-Pavãozinho\") and consists of a single 153 m (502 ft) long line with 5 stations[[313]](#cite_note-316) that carries 4000 passengers per day.[[314]](#cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317) The Penna Church Inclined Elevator is 70 m (230 ft) long[[308]](#cite_note-Penna_Inclined_Elevator_Reform-311) and runs from [Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepaguá \"Jacarepaguá\") to Our Lady of Penna Church at the top of Penna Hill,[[315]](#cite_note-318)[[316]](#cite_note-319) carrying 180 passengers per day.[[314]](#cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317) The Father Laércio Dias de Moura Inclined Elevator is a group of three inclined elevators[[317]](#cite_note-320) that connect [Penha station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penha_Station?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Penha Station (page does not exist)\") to [Vila Cruzeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vila_Cruzeiro?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Vila Cruzeiro (page does not exist)\") and the [Basilica of Our Lady of Penha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Our_Lady_of_Penha?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Basilica of Our Lady of Penha (page does not exist)\");[[318]](#cite_note-321) the inclined elevators have a total length of 426 m (1,398 ft)[[319]](#cite_note-322)[[320]](#cite_note-323) and carry 3000 passengers per day.[[314]](#cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317) The Outeiro da Glória Inclined Elevator is 65 m (213 ft) long and runs from [Glória station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glória_Station \"Glória Station\") to Our Lady of Glória do Outeiro Church, carrying 200 passengers per day.[[321]](#cite_note-324)[[314]](#cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317)\n\n### Roads\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=39 \"Edit section: Roads\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Morro_do_Borel.jpg/250px-Morro_do_Borel.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Morro_do_Borel.jpg)\n\nStreetscape in Rio de Janeiro\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Centro._Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brazil_%2833%29.jpg/250px-Centro._Rio_de_Janeiro%2C_Brazil_%2833%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Centro._Rio_de_Janeiro,_Brazil_(33).jpg)\n\nPedestrian infrastructure in Rio de Janeiro\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/BikeRio_11_2015_Pra%C3%A7a_Mau%C3%A1_706.JPG/250px-BikeRio_11_2015_Pra%C3%A7a_Mau%C3%A1_706.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:BikeRio_11_2015_Praça_Mauá_706.JPG)\n\n[Bike Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_Rio \"Bike Rio\") rental station in [Mauá Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praça_Mauá \"Praça Mauá\"), Downtown Rio\n\nRio de Janeiro has 601 km (373 mi) of [limited-access roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-access_roads \"Limited-access roads\"), 731 km (454 mi) of [arterial roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arterial_roads \"Arterial roads\"), 1,245 km (774 mi) of [collector roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collector_roads \"Collector roads\") and 6,678 km (4,150 mi) of [local roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street \"Street\") with vehicular traffic. Despite representing 72% of roads with vehicular traffic, just 14% of [traffic collisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic_collisions \"Traffic collisions\") and 14% of [traffic fatalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidemiology_of_motor_vehicle_collisions \"Epidemiology of motor vehicle collisions\") are on local roads; 38% of traffic fatalities happen on 20 roads, with [Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Avenida Brasil (Rio de Janeiro)\") alone accounting for 14%. Rio de Janeiro had 694 traffic deaths in 2022, equivalent to 11 deaths per 100,000 inhabitants.[[322]](#cite_note-325)\n\nRio de Janeiro's vehicle fleet includes 3.2 million [motorized vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorized_vehicles \"Motorized vehicles\") - equivalent to 482 per 1000 inhabitants - including 2.2 million [cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cars \"Cars\"), 500,000 [motorcycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorcycles \"Motorcycles\"), 400,000 [trucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trucks \"Trucks\") and 37,000 [buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buses \"Buses\") and [minibuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minibuses \"Minibuses\"). 37% of vehicles use exclusively [E27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethanol_fuel_in_Brazil \"Ethanol fuel in Brazil\") fuel; 6% use E100; 4% use [diesel fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diesel_fuel \"Diesel fuel\"), and 59% use [various combinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multifuel \"Multifuel\") of [ethanol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethanol_fuel \"Ethanol fuel\"), [gasoline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gasoline \"Gasoline\") and [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_vehicle \"Natural gas vehicle\").[[323]](#cite_note-326)[[324]](#cite_note-327) An average of 1.6 million motorized vehicles circulate per day in the city. Approximately 47% of all trips in Rio use public transport; 23% use private motorized vehicles and 28% use [active mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_mobility \"Active mobility\").[[325]](#cite_note-328)[[326]](#cite_note-329)\n\nDespite being in the [Atlantic Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Forest \"Atlantic Forest\"), Rio de Janeiro has a severe lack of [street trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_forestry \"Urban forestry\"). The distribution of the trees that do exist is highly unequal: poorer neighbourhoods on the North side of the city are disproportionately likely to lack tree cover, as are neighbourhoods with higher populations of [pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo_Brazilians \"Pardo Brazilians\") and [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilians \"Afro-Brazilians\") people. Overall, 35% of [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariocas \"Cariocas\") live on streets without any trees at all, and another 26% live on streets with fewer than 5 trees. This [environmental racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_racism \"Environmental racism\") directly causes poorer neighbourhoods to suffer from more extreme weather, including more [severe flooding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_flooding \"Urban flooding\") and [higher temperatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island \"Urban heat island\") - for example, [Lagoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagoa,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Lagoa, Rio de Janeiro\") has a mean income per capita nearly 10 times higher than that of [Del Castilho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Del_Castilho \"Del Castilho\") and has a mean temperature of 26 °C (79 °F), as opposed to Del Castilho's 33 °C (91 °F).[[327]](#cite_note-330)[[328]](#cite_note-331)[[329]](#cite_note-332)\n\nDespite Rio de Janeiro's ample [sidewalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidewalk \"Sidewalk\") coverage, many sidewalks are narrow, inaccessible for [disabled people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disabled_people \"Disabled people\"), and filled with obstacles. [Traffic lights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic_lights \"Traffic lights\") frequently prioritize vehicular traffic on busy roads significantly more than pedestrian traffic, which can significantly increase pedestrian travel times. [Portuguese pavement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_pavement \"Portuguese pavement\"), which is common in Rio de Janeiro,[[330]](#cite_note-333) is extremely slippery in the rain and gets easily damaged by tree roots, which further harms pedestrian accessibility. The city has [pedestrian zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedestrian_zones \"Pedestrian zones\") in the [city center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Centro, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Paquetá Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paquetá_Island \"Paquetá Island\") and concentrated around the [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supervia \"Supervia\") stations in [Bangu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangu,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Bangu, Rio de Janeiro\"), [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Campo Grande, Rio de Janeiro\") and [Penha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penha,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Penha, Rio de Janeiro\"), as well as in [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas \"Favelas\"). Part of the city has a wayfinding system similar to [Legible London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legible_London \"Legible London\").[[331]](#cite_note-334)[[332]](#cite_note-335)[[333]](#cite_note-336)[[334]](#cite_note-337)\n\nRio de Janeiro has approximately 247 km of [shared-use paths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shared-use_path \"Shared-use path\"), 119 km of [sharrows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharrows \"Sharrows\"), 64 km of segregated [cycle tracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycle_track \"Cycle track\"), and 28 km of roadside [bike lanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_lanes \"Bike lanes\"). 16% of [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariocas \"Cariocas\") live within 300 meters of [bicycle infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle_infrastructure \"Bicycle infrastructure\"); the bicycle infrastructure network also reaches 30% of jobs, 16% of schools, and 21% of [health facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_facilities \"Health facilities\"). 127 of the city's 288 medium- and high-capacity public transport ([BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_Rapid_Transit \"Bus Rapid Transit\"), [ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferry \"Ferry\"), [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail \"Light rail\"), [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Transit \"Rapid Transit\") and [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_rail \"Suburban rail\")) stations are served by bicycle infrastructure. Bicycle infrastructure in the city is frequently narrow, with 66% of it failing to reach the city government's minimum standards. The city also has a [bicycle sharing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle_sharing_system \"Bicycle sharing system\") called [Bike Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_Rio \"Bike Rio\") with 430 stations and 6700 bicycles.[[335]](#cite_note-338)[[336]](#cite_note-339)[[337]](#cite_note-340)\n\n#### Major highways\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=40 \"Edit section: Major highways\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/AvenidaBrasilRJ.jpg/250px-AvenidaBrasilRJ.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AvenidaBrasilRJ.jpg)\n\n[Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Avenida Brasil (Rio de Janeiro)\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Bridge_to_Niteroi.jpg/250px-Bridge_to_Niteroi.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bridge_to_Niteroi.jpg)\n\n[Rio-Niterói Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio-Niterói_Bridge \"Rio-Niterói Bridge\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Linha_Vermelha.JPG/250px-Linha_Vermelha.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Linha_Vermelha.JPG)\n\n[Linha Vermelha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Line_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Red Line (Rio de Janeiro)\")\n\n[Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Avenida Brasil (Rio de Janeiro)\") (*Brazil Avenue*) is the longest and busiest [highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-access_road \"Limited-access road\") in Rio de Janeiro, stretching 58 km (36 mi) from the [Port area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Port of Rio de Janeiro\") in the [city center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Zona Central (Rio de Janeiro)\") through the North side to [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Cruz, Rio de Janeiro\") in the West side and carrying 800,000 vehicles per day.[[338]](#cite_note-341)[[339]](#cite_note-342) Almost the entire highway is part of [BR-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-101 \"BR-101\"),[[340]](#cite_note-343) which continues east along the [Rio-Niterói Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio-Niterói_Bridge \"Rio-Niterói Bridge\")[[341]](#cite_note-344) towards [Vitória](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitória,_Espírito_Santo \"Vitória, Espírito Santo\"),[[342]](#cite_note-345) [Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador,_Bahia \"Salvador, Bahia\")[[343]](#cite_note-346) and [Recife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recife \"Recife\")[[344]](#cite_note-347)[[345]](#cite_note-348) and west through [Itaguaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itaguaí \"Itaguaí\")[[346]](#cite_note-349)[[347]](#cite_note-350) towards [Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos,_São_Paulo \"Santos, São Paulo\"),[[348]](#cite_note-351) [Curitiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curitiba \"Curitiba\")[[349]](#cite_note-352) and [Florianópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florianópolis \"Florianópolis\").[[350]](#cite_note-353)[[351]](#cite_note-354) Shorter sections of Avenida Brasil are also part of [BR-040](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-040 \"BR-040\")[[352]](#cite_note-355) (which runs from the Rio de Janeiro Port area[[353]](#cite_note-356) to [Petrópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrópolis \"Petrópolis\"),[[354]](#cite_note-357) [Belo Horizonte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo_Horizonte \"Belo Horizonte\") and [Brasília](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brasília \"Brasília\")),[[355]](#cite_note-358) [BR-116](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-116 \"BR-116\") (which runs northeast from Rio de Janeiro to [Teresópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresópolis \"Teresópolis\"), [Vitória da Conquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitória_da_Conquista \"Vitória da Conquista\") and [Fortaleza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortaleza \"Fortaleza\") and northwest to [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo \"São Paulo\"), Curitiba and [Porto Alegre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_Alegre \"Porto Alegre\"))[[356]](#cite_note-359) and [BR-465](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-465?action=edit&redlink=1 \"BR-465 (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-465 \"pt:BR-465\")] (which runs from the neighbourhood of [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Campo Grande, Rio de Janeiro\") to [Seropédica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seropédica \"Seropédica\")).[[357]](#cite_note-360) Avenida Brasil is a critical freight corridor and bottleneck, particularly for the Port of Rio de Janeiro, with over 500 trucks per day using the highway in the morning peak.[[358]](#cite_note-361)[[359]](#cite_note-362) The 26 km (16 mi) long [Transbrasil BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransBrasil_(bus_rapid_transit) \"TransBrasil (bus rapid transit)\") runs in the middle of Avenida Brasil from [Gentileza Intermodal Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentileza_Intermodal_Terminal \"Gentileza Intermodal Terminal\") to [Deodoro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deodoro_station \"Deodoro station\") and carries 115,000 passengers per day.[[360]](#cite_note-363)[[285]](#cite_note-Transbrasil/Light_Rail_passenger_numbers-288)\n\nThe [Rio-Niterói Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio-Niterói_Bridge \"Rio-Niterói Bridge\") is a 13 km (8 mi) long [box girder bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Box_girder_bridge \"Box girder bridge\") that runs between the [Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Port of Rio de Janeiro\") and the [Port of Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Niterói?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Port of Niterói (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto%20de%20Niterói \"pt:Porto de Niterói\")] via Mocanguê Island. The bridge, which is part of [BR-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-101 \"BR-101\"), is the only direct road connection between the east and west sides of [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay \"Guanabara Bay\") and is therefore a critical traffic bottleneck, carrying 150,000 vehicles per day, including over 7000 trucks. Most of the bridge is 60 m (197 ft) above the water, with the central span rising to 72 m (236 ft) to allow for large ships to pass. There is a [toll booth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toll_booth \"Toll booth\") in the eastbound direction.[[361]](#cite_note-364)[[362]](#cite_note-365)[[363]](#cite_note-366)[[364]](#cite_note-367)[[365]](#cite_note-368)\n\nThe [Linha Amarela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Line_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Yellow Line (Rio de Janeiro)\") (*Yellow Line*) highway, officially *Avenida Governador Carlos Lacerda*, runs 17 km (11 mi) from [Fundão Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Universitária \"Cidade Universitária\") to [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca \"Barra da Tijuca\") via [Del Castilho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Del_Castilho \"Del Castilho\"), [Méier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Méier \"Méier\") and [Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepaguá \"Jacarepaguá\"). It is maintained by LAMSA (Linha Amarela [S.A.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S.A._(corporation) \"S.A. (corporation)\")), which is owned by [Invepar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invepar \"Invepar\"), and is one of two tolled roads within city limits.[[366]](#cite_note-369)[[367]](#cite_note-370)[[368]](#cite_note-371) The Yellow Line carries 120,000 vehicles per day.[[369]](#cite_note-372)[[370]](#cite_note-373)[[371]](#cite_note-374) It runs entirely parallel to the [Transcarioca BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransCarioca \"TransCarioca\").[[372]](#cite_note-375)\n\nThe [Linha Vermelha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Line_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Red Line (Rio de Janeiro)\") (*Red Line*), officially *Via Expressa Presidente João Goulart* or *RJ-071*, is a 22 km (14 mi) long highway that runs from [Cidade Nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Nova,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Cidade Nova, Rio de Janeiro\") to [São João de Meriti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_João_de_Meriti \"São João de Meriti\") via [Fundão Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Universitária \"Cidade Universitária\"), [Galeão International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galeão_International_Airport \"Galeão International Airport\") and [Duque de Caxias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duque_de_Caxias,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Duque de Caxias, Rio de Janeiro\"), entirely parallel to [Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Avenida Brasil (Rio de Janeiro)\").[[373]](#cite_note-376)[[374]](#cite_note-377)[[375]](#cite_note-378) There is an 8 km (5 mi) barrier known as the Wall of Shame along the highway in [Maré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maré,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Maré, Rio de Janeiro\"); this was ostensibly built as a [noise barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noise_barrier \"Noise barrier\") during the leadup to the [2016 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics \"2016 Summer Olympics\") but is widely considered by local residents to be a way for the city to hide the [favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela \"Favela\") from Galeão International Airport passengers.[[376]](#cite_note-379)[[377]](#cite_note-380) Trucks are banned from using the highway.[[378]](#cite_note-381) The Red Line carries 90,000 vehicles per day.[[379]](#cite_note-382)\n\n### Airports\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=41 \"Edit section: Airports\")]\n\nThe following airports serve the city of Rio de Janeiro:\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/Aeroporto_galeao_rj_ME.jpg/250px-Aeroporto_galeao_rj_ME.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aeroporto_galeao_rj_ME.jpg)\n\n[Rio de Janeiro/Galeão International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro/Galeão_International_Airport \"Rio de Janeiro/Galeão International Airport\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Aeroporto_do_Rio_de_Janeiro_RJ_Santos_Dumont_2.jpg/250px-Aeroporto_do_Rio_de_Janeiro_RJ_Santos_Dumont_2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aeroporto_do_Rio_de_Janeiro_RJ_Santos_Dumont_2.jpg)\n\n[Rio de Janeiro–Santos Dumont Domestic Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos_Dumont_Airport \"Santos Dumont Airport\")\n\n* [Galeão–Antônio Carlos Jobim International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro/Galeão_International_Airport \"Rio de Janeiro/Galeão International Airport\"): used for all international and most domestic flights. This airport is connected to local and intercity buses and the [TransCarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransCarioca \"TransCarioca\") and [TransBrasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransBrasil_(bus_rapid_transit) \"TransBrasil (bus rapid transit)\") BRTs.[[380]](#cite_note-383)[[381]](#cite_note-384)[[382]](#cite_note-385)[[383]](#cite_note-386)\n* [Santos Dumont Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos_Dumont_Airport \"Santos Dumont Airport\"): used mainly for services to [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo \"São Paulo\"), some short and medium-haul domestic flights, and [general aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_aviation \"General aviation\").[[384]](#cite_note-387) As of 2025, the airport's passenger capacity is capped by the federal government at 6.5 million per year.[[385]](#cite_note-388) The airport is connected to the city's [light rail system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Light_Rail \"Rio de Janeiro Light Rail\"), which connects it to several other transport modes in the city center.[[386]](#cite_note-389)\n* [Jacarepaguá–Roberto Marinho Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepaguá_Airport \"Jacarepaguá Airport\"): used by general aviation and home to the Aeroclube do Brasil (Brasil [Flying club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_club \"Flying club\")).[[387]](#cite_note-390) It also has some short-haul commercial flights.[[388]](#cite_note-391) The airport is located in the district of [Baixada de Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepaguá \"Jacarepaguá\")[[389]](#cite_note-392) and is connected to the [TransCarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransCarioca \"TransCarioca\") and [TransOlímpica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transolímpica \"Transolímpica\") BRTs.[[390]](#cite_note-393)\n\nAir Force Bases include:\n\n* [Galeão Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galeão_Air_Force_Base \"Galeão Air Force Base\"): A [Brazilian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Air_Force \"Brazilian Air Force\") airbase, sharing some facilities with Galeão - Antônio Carlos Jobim International Airport;\n* [Santa Cruz Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Air_Force_Base \"Santa Cruz Air Force Base\"): A Brazilian Air Force airbase. Formerly called [Bartolomeu de Gusmão Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolomeu_de_Gusmão_Airport \"Bartolomeu de Gusmão Airport\"), it was built by the [Luftschiffbau Zeppelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luftschiffbau_Zeppelin \"Luftschiffbau Zeppelin\"). Today it is one of the most important Air Force Bases in Brazil;\n* [Afonsos Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonsos_Air_Force_Base \"Afonsos Air Force Base\"): One of the historical Brazilian Air Force airbases. It is also the location of the University of the Air Force (Universidade da Força Aérea),[[391]](#cite_note-394) the [Museu Aeroespacial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Aeroespacial \"Museu Aeroespacial\"),[[392]](#cite_note-395) and where air shows take place.\n\n### Ports\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=42 \"Edit section: Ports\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Cruise_Ship_Costa_Serena_docked_in_Rio_de_Janeiro_%28city%29_-_Feb._2011.jpg/250px-Cruise_Ship_Costa_Serena_docked_in_Rio_de_Janeiro_%28city%29_-_Feb._2011.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cruise_Ship_Costa_Serena_docked_in_Rio_de_Janeiro_(city)_-_Feb._2011.jpg)\n\n[Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Port of Rio de Janeiro\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Ilha_D%27%C3%81gua_by_Diego_Baravelli.jpg/250px-Ilha_D%27%C3%81gua_by_Diego_Baravelli.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ilha_D'Água_by_Diego_Baravelli.jpg)\n\nIlha d'Água Terminal\n\nThe [Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Port of Rio de Janeiro\") is Brazil's tenth busiest publicly-owned [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port \"Port\") in terms of cargo volume,[[393]](#cite_note-ANTAQ-396) and it is the main port for [cruise vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruise_ship \"Cruise ship\"). Located in the [Zona Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Zona Central, Rio de Janeiro\") and on the west coast of [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay \"Guanabara Bay\"), it primarily serves the [Southeastern states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Region,_Brazil \"Southeast Region, Brazil\") of [Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_(state) \"Rio de Janeiro (state)\"), [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/São_Paulo_(state) \"São Paulo (state)\"), [Minas Gerais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minas_Gerais \"Minas Gerais\"), and [Espírito Santo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espírito_Santo \"Espírito Santo\"). The port is managed overall by the Companhia Docas do Rio de Janeiro, though private companies operate some parts of it. The Port of Rio de Janeiro stretches from Mauá Pier in the east[[394]](#cite_note-397) to Caju Wharf in the north and includes three [wharves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharves \"Wharves\") with a total length of 6,740 m (22,110 ft). Gamboa Wharf is 3,042 m (9,980 ft) long and includes 20 [berths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berth_(moorings) \"Berth (moorings)\") and 19 [warehouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warehouses \"Warehouses\"); this wharf is shallower than the others and therefore typically handles [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat \"Wheat\") on [Handysize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handysize \"Handysize\") and [Handymax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handymax \"Handymax\") ships. Since Gamboa Wharf includes the cruise ship terminal at Mauá Pier, this also prevents the [largest cruise ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_cruise_ships \"List of largest cruise ships\") from docking in the port. São Cristóvão Wharf is 1,259 m (4,131 ft) long and has 12 berths and two warehouses; the eastern end of this wharf is also shallow enough to restrict shipping operations. Caju Wharf is 2,439 m (8,002 ft) long and includes 6 berths, 7 warehouses and 24 [storage tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storage_tanks \"Storage tanks\"), with 5,000 m3 (180,000 cubic feet) of storage tank space reserved for [sodium hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_hydroxide \"Sodium hydroxide\") and the remaining 12,000 m3 (420,000 cubic feet) reserved for [petroleum products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_products \"Petroleum products\"). There is also a group of storage tanks just outside the port with a total capacity of 22,000 tonnes and pipelines that connect them to São Cristóvão Wharf. The berths at Caju Wharf are large enough for ships of up to 349 m (1,145 ft) [length overall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Length_overall \"Length overall\").[[395]](#cite_note-398)[[396]](#cite_note-Port_Master_Plan-399)\n\nIn 2024, the Port of Rio de Janeiro handled 15.5 million tonnes of cargo, 11.8 million tonnes of which were [containerized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containerized \"Containerized\") into 937,000 [TEUs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-foot_equivalent_unit \"Twenty-foot equivalent unit\"). In addition to container cargo, the port also handled 1.1 million tonnes of [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron \"Iron\") and [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel \"Steel\") products, 873,000 tonnes of [petroleum products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_products \"Petroleum products\") (not including [crude oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crude_oil \"Crude oil\")), 444,000 tonnes of [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat \"Wheat\"), 370,000 tonnes of crude oil and 205,000 tonnes of [motor vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_vehicles \"Motor vehicles\"). 10.2 million tonnes of cargo were moved internationally, 5.2 million tonnes were moved via [short-sea shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-sea_shipping \"Short-sea shipping\"), and 0.075 million tonnes were moved through [inland navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inland_navigation \"Inland navigation\").[[393]](#cite_note-ANTAQ-396) The Port of Rio de Janeiro also moved 327,000 passengers on 36 [cruise ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruise_ship \"Cruise ship\") making 107 calls during the 2024/25 cruise season.[[397]](#cite_note-400)\n\nIn addition to the Port of Rio de Janeiro, the city also has ten private port facilities, nine of which are on [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay \"Guanabara Bay\") and one of which is on [Sepetiba Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepetiba_Bay?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Sepetiba Bay (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baía%20de%20Sepetiba \"pt:Baía de Sepetiba\")]. The Guanabara Bay port facilities collectively handled 16.6 million tonnes of [petroleum products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_products \"Petroleum products\") in 2024 and no other cargo. 15.8 million tonnes of this cargo were shipped through the Ilha d'Água Terminal, which is owned by [Transpetro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transpetro \"Transpetro\") and has a pipeline connected to [REDUC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/REDUC?action=edit&redlink=1 \"REDUC (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/REDUC \"pt:REDUC\")] ([Duque de Caxias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duque_de_Caxias,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Duque de Caxias, Rio de Janeiro\") Refinery).[[398]](#cite_note-401)[[399]](#cite_note-402) 14 million tonnes of cargo were moved through [short-sea shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-sea_shipping \"Short-sea shipping\") and 2.6 million tonnes were moved internationally. The port on Sepetiba Bay, which is owned by [Ternium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternium \"Ternium\"), moved 7.3 million tonnes of cargo in 2024 to support its [steel mill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_mill \"Steel mill\") in [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Santa Cruz, Rio de Janeiro\"), almost all of it internationally. This port moved 3.2 million tonnes of [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron \"Iron\") and [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel \"Steel\"), 2.4 million tonnes of [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal \"Coal\"), 0.8 million tonnes of [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone \"Limestone\"), 0.4 million tonnes of [mineral oils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_oil \"Mineral oil\"), 0.4 million tonnes of [iron ore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_ore \"Iron ore\") and 0.2 million tonnes of [petroleum coke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_coke \"Petroleum coke\") in 2024.[[393]](#cite_note-ANTAQ-396)[[396]](#cite_note-Port_Master_Plan-399)[[400]](#cite_note-403)\n\n### Water and sewage\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=43 \"Edit section: Water and sewage\")]\n\nFurther information: [Water supply and sanitation in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Brazil \"Water supply and sanitation in Brazil\")\n\nThe water supply of Rio de Janeiro and the [Baixada Fluminense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baixada_Fluminense \"Baixada Fluminense\") is sourced through the interconnected Guandu, Ribeirão das Lajes and Acari [water supply systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_system \"Water supply system\"). The Guandu water supply system, which uses water [diverted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interbasin_transfer \"Interbasin transfer\") from the [Paraíba do Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraíba_do_Sul \"Paraíba do Sul\") and [Piraí Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraí_River_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Piraí River (Rio de Janeiro)\") to the [Guandu River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guandu_River_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Guandu River (Rio de Janeiro)\"), includes the largest [water treatment plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_treatment_plant \"Water treatment plant\") in the world, with a throughput of 43 m3 (1,500 cubic feet) per second.[[401]](#cite_note-404) The Ribeirão das Lajes system uses water diverted from the Piraí River (but not the [Paraíba do Sul River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraíba_do_Sul_River \"Paraíba do Sul River\")) to the [Ribeirão das Lajes River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribeirão_das_Lajes \"Ribeirão das Lajes\") and supplies 5.1 m3 (180 cubic feet) of water per second.[[402]](#cite_note-405) The Acari system uses five [raw water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raw_water \"Raw water\") collection points in the [Tinguá Biological Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinguá_Biological_Reserve \"Tinguá Biological Reserve\") and supplies 3 m3 (110 cubic feet) of water per second.[[403]](#cite_note-406) Rio de Janeiro's [water security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_security \"Water security\") is threatened by its dependence on a single source, [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change \"Climate change\") and raw water pollution, particularly in the Guandu River.[[404]](#cite_note-407)[[405]](#cite_note-Imunana-Laranjal-408)[[406]](#cite_note-409)\n\n[Paquetá Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paquetá_Island \"Paquetá Island\"), unlike the rest of Rio de Janeiro City, has its water supplied from the Imunana-Laranjal [water supply system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_system \"Water supply system\"), which uses water sourced from the [Macacu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macacu_River \"Macacu River\") and [Guapiaçú Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guapiaçú_River \"Guapiaçú River\") and also supplies the [Leste Fluminense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leste_Fluminense?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Leste Fluminense (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leste%20Fluminense \"pt:Leste Fluminense\")]. This system treats 7 m3 (250 cubic feet) of water per second.[[407]](#cite_note-410)[[405]](#cite_note-Imunana-Laranjal-408)\n\nNational policy changes in 2020–2021 for the universalization of sanitation kept the 2007 policy defining the municipality as the provider of sanitation service, delegating the organization, supervision, and provision of services to third parties.[[408]](#cite_note-411)[[409]](#cite_note-sanitation-412) The [National Water and Basic Sanitation Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Water_and_Basic_Sanitation_Agency?action=edit&redlink=1 \"National Water and Basic Sanitation Agency (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agência%20Nacional%20de%20Águas%20e%20Saneamento%20Básico \"pt:Agência Nacional de Águas e Saneamento Básico\")] (ANA) is responsible for the setting standards regulating basic public sanitation services.[[409]](#cite_note-sanitation-412) In 2021–2022, distribution and treatment of sewage of the [Rio de Janeiro State Water and Sewage Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_Water_and_Sewage_Company?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Rio de Janeiro State Water and Sewage Company (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Companhia%20Estadual%20de%20Águas%20e%20Esgotos%20do%20Rio%20de%20Janeiro \"pt:Companhia Estadual de Águas e Esgotos do Rio de Janeiro\")] (CEDAE) was divided into four blocks and auctioned to Aegea (Águas do Rio) and Iguá; CEDAE remained in control of water collection and treatment of drinking water.[[410]](#cite_note-privatizes-water-treatment-413)[[411]](#cite_note-414) The [Rio de Janeiro State Environmental Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_Environmental_Institute?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Rio de Janeiro State Environmental Institute (page does not exist)\") [[pt](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto%20Estadual%20do%20Ambiente%20(Rio%20de%20Janeiro) \"pt:Instituto Estadual do Ambiente (Rio de Janeiro)\")] (INEA) has primary responsibility for water pollution monitoring and enforcement.[[412]](#cite_note-degradation-415)\n\nAs of 2021[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit) only 65% of sewage was properly treated, leaving 35% to be improperly discharged.[[410]](#cite_note-privatizes-water-treatment-413) As of 2022[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit) there were at least 400 illicit sewage disposal points in the drainage network.[[412]](#cite_note-degradation-415)\n\n### Solid waste\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=44 \"Edit section: Solid waste\")]\n\n[Solid waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_waste \"Solid waste\") management in Rio de Janeiro is done by the Companhia Municipal de Limpeza Urbana (*Municipal Urban Cleaning Company* or *Comlurb*), which is the largest municipal [waste management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_management \"Waste management\") company in [Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_America \"Latin America\").[[413]](#cite_note-416) Rio de Janeiro produces over 3.2 million tonnes of solid waste per year. After being collected throughout the city, solid waste is sent through one of five [waste transfer stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_transfer_station \"Waste transfer station\") and then on to a [sanitary landfill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitary_landfill \"Sanitary landfill\") in [Seropédica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seropédica \"Seropédica\"),[[414]](#cite_note-417) which produces 24,000 m³ of [biogas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogas \"Biogas\") and 5,000 m³ of [biomethane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomethane \"Biomethane\") per hour; [leachate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leachate \"Leachate\") is also treated and used to produce over 60m³ of [distilled water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distilled_water \"Distilled water\") per hour.[[415]](#cite_note-418) Despite 40% of the city's waste being made of recyclable materials, only 2% of it is actually [recycled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recycled \"Recycled\").[[416]](#cite_note-419)\n\n## Culture\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=45 \"Edit section: Culture\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/MAM_-_Museu_de_Arte_Moderna_do_Rio_de_Janeiro_02.jpg/250px-MAM_-_Museu_de_Arte_Moderna_do_Rio_de_Janeiro_02.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MAM_-_Museu_de_Arte_Moderna_do_Rio_de_Janeiro_02.jpg)\n\n[Museum of Modern Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Modern_Art,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Museum of Modern Art, Rio de Janeiro\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Esculturas%2C_Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes_02.jpg/250px-Esculturas%2C_Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes_02.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Esculturas,_Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes_02.jpg)\n\n[National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes \"Museu Nacional de Belas Artes\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Rio_de_Janeiro_-_Cristo_Redentor_01.jpg/250px-Rio_de_Janeiro_-_Cristo_Redentor_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rio_de_Janeiro_-_Cristo_Redentor_01.jpg)\n\n*Christ the Redeemer*\n\nRio de Janeiro is a main cultural hub in Brazil. Its architecture embraces churches and buildings dating from the 16th to the 19th centuries, blending with the world-renowned designs of the 20th century. Rio was home to the [Portuguese Imperial family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal \"Portugal\") and capital of the country for many years, and was influenced by [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Portugal \"Architecture of Portugal\"), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_England \"Architecture of England\"), and [French architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_architecture \"French architecture\").[[417]](#cite_note-420)\n\nRio de Janeiro has inherited a strong cultural role from the past. In the late 19th century, there were sessions held of the first Brazilian film, and since then, several production cycles have spread out, eventually placing Rio at the forefront of experimental and national cinema. The [Rio de Janeiro International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_International_Film_Festival \"Rio de Janeiro International Film Festival\")[[418]](#cite_note-421) has been held annually since 1999.[[419]](#cite_note-422)\n\nRio currently brings together the main production centers of Brazilian television.[[420]](#cite_note-423) Major international films set in Rio de Janeiro include *[Blame it on Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blame_it_on_Rio \"Blame it on Rio\")*; the [James Bond film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bond_in_film \"James Bond in film\") [*Moonraker*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonraker_(film) \"Moonraker (film)\"); the Oscar award-winning, critically acclaimed *[Central Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Station_(film) \"Central Station (film)\")* by [Walter Salles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Salles \"Walter Salles\"), who is also one of Brazil's best-known directors; and the Oscar award-winning historical drama, *[Black Orpheus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Orpheus \"Black Orpheus\")*, which depicted the early days of Carnaval in Rio de Janeiro. Internationally distributed Brazilian-made movies illustrating a darker side of Rio de Janeiro include *[Elite Squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elite_Squad \"Elite Squad\")* and [*City of God*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_God_(2002_film) \"City of God (2002 film)\").\n\nRio has many important cultural landmarks, such as the Biblioteca Nacional (National Library), one of the largest libraries in the world with collections totaling more than 9 million items; the [Theatro Municipal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Municipal_(Rio_de_Janeiro) \"Teatro Municipal (Rio de Janeiro)\"); the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes \"Museu Nacional de Belas Artes\"); the [Carmen Miranda Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmen_Miranda_Museum \"Carmen Miranda Museum\"); the [Rio de Janeiro Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Botanical_Garden \"Rio de Janeiro Botanical Garden\"); the [Parque Lage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Lage \"Parque Lage\"); the [Quinta da Boa Vista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinta_da_Boa_Vista \"Quinta da Boa Vista\"); the [Imperial Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paço_Imperial \"Paço Imperial\"); the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras \"Academia Brasileira de Letras\"); the [Museu de Arte Moderna do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Modern_Art,_Rio_de_Janeiro \"Museum of Modern Art, Rio de Janeiro\"); and the [Natural History Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Brazil \"National Museum of Brazil\").\n\n### Literature\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=46 \"Edit section: Literature\")]\n\nMain article: [Literature of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_of_Brazil \"Literature of Brazil\")\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/AcademiaBrasileira1.jpg/250px-AcademiaBrasileira1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AcademiaBrasileira1.jpg)\n\n[Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras \"Academia Brasileira de Letras\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Biblioteca_nacional_rio_janeiro.jpg/250px-Biblioteca_nacional_rio_janeiro.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Biblioteca_nacional_rio_janeiro.jpg)\n\n[National Library of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil \"National Library of Brazil\")\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/%E3%83%AA%E3%82%AA%E3%81%AE%E5%B9%BB%E6%83%B3%E5%9B%B3%E6%9B%B8%E9%A4%A8_Real_Gabinete_Portugues_de_Leitura_%288735773218%29.jpg/250px-%E3%83%AA%E3%82%AA%E3%81%AE%E5%B9%BB%E6%83%B3%E5%9B%B3%E6%9B%B8%E9%A4%A8_Real_Gabinete_Portugues_de_Leitura_%288735773218%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:リオの幻想図書館_Real_Gabinete_Portugues_de_Leitura_(8735773218).jpg)\n\n[Royal Portuguese Cabinet of Reading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Portuguese_Cabinet_of_Reading \"Royal Portuguese Cabinet of Reading\")\n\nAfter Brazilian independence from Portugal in 1822, Rio de Janeiro quickly developed a [European-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Europe \"Culture of Europe\") bourgeois cultural life, including numerous newspapers, in which most 19th-century novels were initially published in serial form. [Joaquim Manuel de Macedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaquim_Manuel_de_Macedo \"Joaquim Manuel de Macedo\")'s *[A Moreninha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Moreninha_(novel) \"A Moreninha (novel)\")* (1844) was perhaps the first successful novel in Brazil and inaugurates a recurrent 19th-century theme: a romantic relationship between idealistic young people in spite of the cruelties of social fortune.\n\nThe first notable work of realism focusing on the urban [lower-middle class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_middle_class \"Lower middle class\") is [Manuel Antônio de Almeida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Antônio_de_Almeida \"Manuel Antônio de Almeida\")'s *[Memórias de um sargento de milícias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memórias_de_um_sargento_de_milícias \"Memórias de um sargento de milícias\")* (1854), which presents a series of picaresque scenes and evokes the transformation of a town into a city with suggestive nostalgia. Romantic and realist modes both flourished through the late 19th century and often overlapped within works.[[421]](#cite_note-424)\n\n[Machado de Assis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machado_de_Assis \"Machado de Assis\") is from Rio de Janeiro, and is widely regarded as the greatest writer of [Brazilian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_literature \"Brazilian literature\")[[422]](#cite_note-425) and considered the founder of [Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_(arts) \"Realism (arts)\") in Brazil, with the publication of *[The Posthumous Memoirs of Bras Cubas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Posthumous_Memoirs_of_Bras_Cubas \"The Posthumous Memoirs of Bras Cubas\")* (1881).[[423]](#cite_note-426) He commented on and criticized the political and social events of the city and country, such as the abolition of slavery in 1888 and the transition from [Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil \"Empire of Brazil\") to Republic, with his numerous [chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicle \"Chronicle\") published in newspapers of the time.[[424]](#cite_note-427) Many of his short stories and novels, like *[Quincas Borba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincas_Borba \"Quincas Borba\")* (1891) and *[Dom Casmurro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dom_Casmurro \"Dom Casmurro\")* (1899), are placed in Rio. The headquarters of the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras \"Academia Brasileira de Letras\") is based in Rio de Janeiro. It was satirized by the novelist [Jorge Amado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Amado \"Jorge Amado\") in *[Pen, Sword, Camisole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pen,_Sword,_Camisole \"Pen, Sword, Camisole\")*. Amado himself went on to be one of the 40 members of the academy.\n\nThe Biblioteca Nacional ([National Library of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil \"National Library of Brazil\")) ranks as one of the largest libraries in the world. It is also the largest library in all of Latin America.[[425]](#cite_note-428) Located in [Cinelndia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinelndia \"Cinelndia\"), the National Library was created by the King of Portugal, in 1810. As with many of Rio de Janeiro's cultural monuments, the library was originally off-limits to the general public. The most valuable collections in the library include: 4,300 items donated by Barbosa Machado including a precious collection of rare brochures detailing the [History of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal \"History of Portugal\") and [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Brazil \"History of Brazil\"); 2,365 items from the 17th and 18th centuries that were previously owned by Antônio de Araújo de Azevedo, the \"Count of Barca\", including the 125-volume set of prints \"Le Grand Thétre de l'Univers;\" a collection of documents regarding the Jesuítica Province of Paraguay and the \"Region of Prata;\" and the Teresa Cristina Maria Collection, donated by [Emperor Pedro II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_II_of_Brazil \"Pedro II of Brazil\"). The collection contains 48,236 items. Individual items of special interest include a rare first edition of *[Os Lusíadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Os_Lusíadas \"Os Lusíadas\")* by [Luis de Camões](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luís_de_Camões \"Luís de Camões\"), published in 1584; two copies of the Mogúncia Bible; and a first edition of Handel's Messiah.[[426]](#cite_note-429)\n\nThe Real Gabinete Português de Leitura (Portuguese Royal Reading Library) is located at Rua Luís de Camões, in the Centro (Downtown). The institution was founded in 1837 by a group of forty-three [Portuguese immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people \"Portuguese people\"), [political refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_refugee \"Political refugee\"), to promote culture among the Portuguese community in the then capital of the Empire. The history of the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras \"Academia Brasileira de Letras\") is linked to the Real Gabinete, since some of the early meetings of the academy were held there.[[427]](#cite_note-430)\n\n### Music\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rio_de_Janeiro&action=edit&section=47 \"Edit section: Music\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Tom_Jobim%2C_1972.tif/lossless-page1-250px-Tom_Jobim%2C_1972.tif.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tom_Jobim,_1972.tif)\n\n[Tom Jobim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Jobim \"Tom Jobim\") in 1972. Considered one of the great exponents of Brazilian music, Jobim internationalized [bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova \"Bossa nova\") with the help of important American artists.\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/TIM_MAIA_SONIA_D%27ALMEIDA_1987.jpg/250px-TIM_MAIA_SONIA_D%27ALMEIDA_1987.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TIM_MAIA_SONIA_D'ALMEIDA_1987.jpg)\n\n[Tim Maia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Maia \"Tim Maia\"), the greatest representative of [soul music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music \"Soul music\") in the country's history, from Rio de Janeiro\n\nThe official song of Rio de Janeiro is \"[Cidade Maravilhosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Maravilhosa \"Cidade Maravilhosa\")\", which means \"marvelous city\". The song is considered the civic anthem of Rio, and is always the favorite song during Rio's [Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Carnival \"Brazilian Carnival\") in February. Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo are considered the center of the [urban music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_contemporary \"Urban contemporary\") movement in Brazil.[[428]](#cite_note-431)\n\n\"Rio was popularized by the hit song \"[The Girl from Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Girl_from_Ipanema \"The Girl from Ipanema\")\", composed by [Antônio Carlos Jobim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antônio_Carlos_Jobim \"Antônio Carlos Jobim\") and [Vinicius de Moraes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinicius_de_Moraes \"Vinicius de Moraes\") and recorded by [Astrud Gilberto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrud_Gilberto \"Astrud Gilberto\") and [João Gilberto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/João_Gilberto \"João Gilberto\"), [Frank Sinatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Sinatra \"Frank Sinatra\"), and [Ella Fitzgerald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Fitzgerald \"Ella Fitzgerald\"). It is also the main key song of the [bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova \"Bossa nova\"), a [music genre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_genre \"Music genre\") born in Rio. A genre unique to Rio and Brazil as a whole is [Funk Carioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk_carioca \"Funk carioca\"). While [samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba \"Samba\") music continues to act as the national unifying agent in Rio, Funk Carioca found a strong community following in Brazil. With its genesis in the 1970s as the modern black pop music from the United States, it evolved in the 1990s to describe a variety of [electronic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_music \"Electronic music\") associated with the current US [black music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_music \"African-American music\") scene, including [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop \"Hip hop\"), modern soul, and [house music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_music \"House music\").\"[[429]](#cite_note-432)\n\nBrazil's return to democracy in 1985 allowed for a new music expression which promoted creativity and experimentation in expressive culture, in a wave of Rock'n'roll that swept the 80s. [Lobão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobão_(musician) \"Lobão (musician)\") emerged as the most legendary rocker in Brazil.[[430]](#cite_note-433) Commercial and cultural imports from Europe and North America have often influenced Brazil's own cultural output. For example, the hip hop that has stemmed from New York is localized into forms of musical production such as Funk Carioca and [Brazilian hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_hip_hop \"Brazilian hip hop\"). Bands from Rio de Janeiro also influenced the mid-to-late development of the [Punk in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_in_Brazil \"Punk in Brazil\"), and that of [Brazilian thrash metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_thrash_metal \"Brazilian thrash metal\"). Democratic renewal also allowed for the recognition and acceptance of this diversification of Brazilian culture.[[431]](#cite_note-434)\n\nSome of the best artists in the history of Brazilian popular music hail from Rio de Janeiro, including aforementioned Lobão, [Tim Maia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Maia \"Tim Maia\"), [Agepê](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agepê \"Agepê\"), [Emílio Santiago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emílio_Santiago \"Emílio Santiago\"), [Carlos Lyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Lyra \"Carlos Lyra\"), [Sandra de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandra_de_Sá \"Sandra de Sá\"), [Erasmo Carlos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmo_Carlos \"Erasmo Carlos\"), [Wilson Simonal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_Simonal \"Wilson Simonal\"), [Cazuza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cazuza \"Cazuza\"), [Ivan Lins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Lins \"Ivan
